#5138. S&P 500 Plunges, Wall Street Fears Circuit Breaker, Just One Day After Trump Celebrated Record Highs

S&P 500 Plunges, Wall Street Fears Circuit Breaker, Just One Day After Trump Celebrated Record Highs

President Donald Trump's celebration of Wall Street's highs aged rather poorly. Less than 24 hours after boasting about Americans' retirement accounts going "through the roof," the S&P 500 plunged as much as 3.8% Thursday, bringing emergency trading halts into the conversation.

The selloff prompted traders to monitor the market's circuit-breaker thresholds, which can temporarily shut down trading across U.S. stock exchanges when losses become too severe. The S&P 500 would need to fall another 3.2 percentage points from its decline to trigger the first emergency halt.

The timing is particularly awkward for Trump, who has repeatedly pointed to stock market performance as evidence of his administration's economic success.

"Stock Market just hit another Record High—91 Days in just a short period of time—Less than two years. Your 401(k)s are through the roof," Trump wrote Wednesday on his Truth Social account. He closed the message with "Record Setting All! President DONALD J. TRUMP."

Wall Street had rallied Wednesday following the Democratic Party's sweeping midterm election victories. The S&P 500 gained 1.6%, its strongest daily performance in three months, while the Dow Jones Industrial Average jumped more than 1,500 points to 53.361 points.

Thursday's losses erase that rally in a single session, with technology stocks taking the biggest beating.

The U.S. stock market has three emergency thresholds designed to slow panic selling. These mechanisms, known as circuit breakers, automatically suspend trading when the S&P 500 falls by a specified percentage from its previous closing price.

The first threshold is a 7% decline, corresponding to an S&P 500 level of 7,150.41 points for Thursday's session. Reaching that level before 3:25 p.m. ET would trigger a 15-minute halt across US equity markets.

The circuit breaker level is located in the following chart’s blue line

A 13% decline, or 6,689.09 points, would trigger another 15-minute suspension. The most severe threshold comes at 20%, or 6,150.89 points, which would shut down trading for the remainder of the day.

Under the New York Stock Exchange's rules, the first two circuit breakers stop applying at 3:25 p.m. ET, while the 20% threshold remains active throughout the session.

The rules apply to the entire U.S. equity market, not just stocks included in the S&P 500. A market-wide halt would also suspend trading in companies listed on the Nasdaq and other US exchanges.

The technology sector was particularly hard hit as investors continued digesting the consequences of Tuesday's election results. At around 11 a.m. ET, the Nasdaq Composite had fallen nearly 5%, while the S&P 500 was down 3.2%.

Nvidia lost more than 6%, while the broader semiconductor index plunged nearly 11%. Companies involved in building AI infrastructure were also under pressure as investors worried that Democratic victories could bring stricter oversight of data-center construction.

"We suspect there is going to be some scrutiny on this aggressive AI buildout," Ipek Ozkardeskaya, senior analyst at Swissquote Bank, told Reuters.

The selloff also spilled into cryptocurrencies. Bitcoin fell below $76,000, wiping out its post-election recovery as approximately $428 million in leveraged crypto long positions were liquidated within an hour, as Decrypt reported earlier today.

Stocks also faced pressure from persistent inflation concerns and uncertainty over the Federal Reserve's next interest-rate decision. Investors are awaiting Friday's October employment report, which could provide further clues about the direction of monetary policy.

Despite Thursday's sharp losses, the S&P 500 had not reached its first market-wide circuit breaker at the time of this report.

#5137. Dogecoin Cofounder Highlights Shiba Inu Popularity in Japan

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Dogecoin cofounder Billy Markus shared a lighthearted observation about Japan's unique dog culture in a post on X, pointing out the popularity of Shiba Inu dogs in Japan.

"Like, 50% of the dogs I see in Japan are Shiba Inu," wrote Markus, who goes by the online handle Shibetoshi Nakamoto.

like 50% of the dogs i see in japan are shiba inu i love japan

The Dogecoin cofounder's comment is based on his own observation when he was in Japan, where the Shiba Inu breed has deep cultural roots and is still one of the country's most recognizable native dog breeds. Markus' observation is noteworthy, as he is the cofounder of Dogecoin, a cryptocurrency that draws its origins from the Shiba Inu dog.

On December 6, 2013, Dogecoin was launched, taking influence from the famous Doge meme: an image of a very adorable Shiba Inu dog with multi-colored Comic Sans text that appears as the dog's "thought bubbles."

This famous meme was based on a 2010 photograph of a Japanese female Shiba Inu dog, Kabosu, adopted by Japanese kindergarten teacher Atsuko Satō from an animal shelter in 2008. This helped establish the dog-themed cryptocurrency trend that later inspired several other tokens, including Shiba Inu (SHIB).

Kabosu, a Japanese Shiba Inu, inspired a generation of online jokes and became the face of Dogecoin cryptocurrency. Kabosu was the face of the breezy Doge meme, her quizzical expression surrounded by an inner monologue of choppy phrases in bad grammar written in rainbow-colored Comic Sans font.

In 2010, two years after adopting Kabosu from a puppy mill where she would otherwise have been put down, Ms. Satō, a teacher from Sakura, east of Tokyo, took a picture of her pet crossing her paws on the sofa. She posted the image on her blog, from where it spread to online forum Reddit and became a meme that bounced from college bedrooms to office email chains.

This inspired Dogecoin, which was started as a joke by two software engineers, Billy Markus and Jackson Palmer, and is now the 12th largest cryptocurrency, with a market capitalization of $11.17 billion, according to CoinMarketCap data.

Kabosu, the dog behind the "doge" meme, died in May 2024 after 14 years of internet fame.

#5136. Trump-Backed American Bitcoin Turns a Profit as Bitcoin Rise Lifts Books

Trump-Backed American Bitcoin Turns a Profit as Bitcoin Rise Lifts Books

American Bitcoin, the Trump-family-backed mining venture, swung to a roughly $126 million profit in the third quarter, a sharp reversal from the loss it posted three months earlier, as a jump in Bitcoin’s book value and disciplined accumulation powered its best showing yet.

The Nasdaq-listed company, which trades under the ticker ABTC and is majority-owned by miner Hut 8, reported net income of about $126 million for the quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with a $57.2 million net loss in the second quarter.

Adjusted EBITDA came in at roughly $221 million, versus a $45 million loss the prior quarter. Much of the swing came from a $197 million gain on its digital assets, as Bitcoin’s value rose during the period.

The company grew its Bitcoin reserve to about 8,427 BTC, up roughly 425 coins, or 5%, from the end of June, and lifted its “satoshis per share,” a measure of how much Bitcoin backs each share, by about 5%. Notably, it did so without buying any Bitcoin or issuing new shares through its at-the-market program during the quarter, relying instead on its own mining output.

“These are significant results, particularly in a challenging digital asset market,” said Eric Trump, the company’s co-founder and chief strategy officer, touting the profit and the growth in Bitcoin per share. CEO Mike Ho credited “a disciplined, Bitcoin-denominated operating business,” saying the firm continued “mining Bitcoin at a structural discount to spot.”

The mining operation itself had a tougher quarter. American Bitcoin mined about 891 BTC, down from 932 in the prior period, and its cost to mine rose to roughly $42,100 per Bitcoin from $36,500, which it attributed to higher seasonal power costs. Gross margin slipped to 41% from 49%. The company ended the quarter with a fleet of about 89,242 miners and 28.1 exahashes per second of capacity.

The results cap a volatile stretch for the venture. American Bitcoin notched a record mining quarter earlier this year as it crossed 8,000 BTC, but its stock has struggled, hitting new lows and prompting a 1-for-15 reverse stock split in July. Shares have fallen well below their debut price since the company went public through a merger with Gryphon Digital Mining.

The company’s profit, while striking, rests heavily on an accounting gain tied to Bitcoin’s price rather than core mining operations, meaning a downturn in Bitcoin could just as easily swing the figure back into the red.

#5135. Vitalik Buterin Declares Ethereum (ETH) a Store of Value, Not Fee-Burning Token

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Ethereum's intrinsic economics is rebuilding itself in motion with tech bugs and the macroeconomic picture overlaying each other in the span of 48 hours. The week wraps up for a major alternative crypto project with a sudden Glamsterdam hard fork stop after testnet launch revealed a dangerous bug in the fee recalculating mechanism, brutally breaking the logic in smart contracts.

As the Ethereum community debated the ability of the network to sustain on old power, Vitalik Buterin showed up with a manifesto which completely zeroes out all the panic around mainnet's decreasing revenue. The "red line" behind Vitalik's updated vision is that the community should stop being fixated on the idea of ETH as "fee-burninng token". For a basic blockchain layer worth $270 billion, attempts to build evaluation on the amount of gas burned are a dead end. For Vitalik, the novel of "ETH as store of value (SoV)" is much more viable amid all the global uncertainty around.

This U-turn, taken by a key visionary of the ecosystem, happened at $2,240 for ETH and may drastically change all the plans developed with a focus on Glamsterdam architecture. The hard fork was initially projected for the logic of blockspace surplus, not the deficit. Ecosystem builders are already readying expansion of L1 capacity with the help of EIP-8037 (tariffs calibration) and EIP-7928 (parallel production) with an aim to raise gas limits up to 200 million units. The logic is that the excess of free space will cheapen the transactions, while token burning rates will gradually be cut.

The market saw a risk of deflation in this concept. Vitalik took a front-step. By his estimates, the historical peaks of burning through EIP-1559 "never were as strong as they seemed". They were dependent on inner cyclical activity of crypto speculations. And it itself was falling once every bear market started.

EIP-1559 is not going anywhere, and Vitalik openly says "don't be sad" if the volume of gas burned will forever stay below the 10% mark. The source of value for Ethereum lies in completely another dimension.

Solana's co-founder, Anatoly Yakovenko, unexpectedly aligned with Buterin on this matter. "You can't focus on fees as the products", he said, calling such an idea "absolutely meaningless". According to Yakovenko, the blockchain network must generate real value for the end users, not control the price of getting into the block.

Vitalik is absolutely right that you can’t focus on fees as the product. That’s absolutely meaningless. You need to pick a product, the thing that brings value to people, and do that really well.

There is lots of technical stuff and philosophy around, but what the missed deadline on the testnet and Vitalik's manifesto reveal is that Ethereum opts for long-term, secure, and even a bit boring stability of the basic layer. Infra doesn't try to flirt with the market through deflationary records.

#5134. Binance’s $6.3B hack leaves questions over hot wallet safeguards

Experts say blockchain records show repeated wallet drains but cannot establish Binance’s internal signing controls or transaction limits.

Binance’s $6.3 billion security incident has left unanswered questions about how the exchange managed its hot wallets and limited the amount attackers could access.

Binance co-CEO Richard Teng said on Oct. 30 that the attack affected the exchange’s own hot wallet. Most customer assets were held in offline cold storage and moved to hot wallets only when users requested withdrawals.

Wallet balances offer only a partial picture of an exchange’s exposure to an attack. Public blockchain records can show funds moving but cannot establish the internal controls governing those transfers, CertiK analyst Jonathan Riss told Cointelegraph.

In its initial report Tuesday, Binance said attackers accessed an internal wallet-signing service to move funds outside its normal withdrawal process. Binance said some technical details would remain confidential to prevent further attacks.

The exchange did not respond to Cointelegraph’s questions about its wallet balances and safeguards.

Rivals including Coinbase, Gate and Upbit have disclosed their cold-storage ratios following the attack.

Riss said the $6.3 billion figure represented the cumulative value of assets affected across 10 blockchains. On Ethereum, attackers repeatedly drained a hot wallet as new deposits arrived.

When attackers can obtain valid signatures, their potential access depends on what the signing system allows them to transfer over time, he said. A wallet’s balance at a single moment does not capture that exposure.

Related: EIP-7702 didn’t cause Binance hack, but may have amplified losses

Transaction limits enforced by the application requesting a signature can be bypassed if that application is compromised, Riss said. Limits enforced independently by the signing system may still restrict transfers.

“A comparison with other exchanges isn’t really possible from the outside,” Riss added. “Public information on internal signing architecture is limited everywhere, and reserve distribution and proof-of-reserves attestations show what sits where, not how a withdrawal is authorized.”

BitGo chief product officer Eugene Hahr also declined to speculate about Binance’s internal safeguards.

“The breakdown of custody arrangements is a question Binance is best placed to answer,” he said.

Idan Ofrat, co-founder and chief product officer at Fireblocks, said exchanges typically keep enough funds in hot wallets to cover a few days of expected withdrawals, often representing a low single-digit percentage of assets under management.

Exchanges generally hold the bulk of customer assets in cold storage, while some use intermediate wallets that require manual approval for transfers to hot wallets, he said.

Related: Binance traces $6.3B hack to fake job interviews, compromised AI tool

Alex Tobol, chief technology officer at Telegram-based crypto wallet Walt, said holding billions of dollars in hot wallets is not inherently unusual for an exchange of Binance’s size.

Hot wallets generally process withdrawals automatically, Tobol said, but replenishing them from cold storage should require a separate treasury decision and manual approval.

Hahr said controls applied before signing, including transaction limits, approved withdrawal addresses and additional authorization requirements, can help contain losses when a system is compromised.

Magazine: Binance hackers raced to move billions before it could be frozen

#5133. Flare CEO Pushes Back Against XRP 'FUD'

Flare CEO Hugo Philion has expressed his frustration about recent fear, uncertainty, and doubt (FUD) around XRP and reiterated that the future of the cryptocurrency should not be centered solely around Ripple.

The Flare CEO recently addressed in an X post the constant habit of XRP holders to evaluate the future of the cryptocurrency through the prism of what Ripple is doing and what it isn't.

"The value and narrative of XRP should never have been viewed as being dependent on the actions of a single actor," the Flare CEO said.

The Flare CEO emphasized that XRP holders should instead concentrate their efforts on finding ways to improve the usability of the cryptocurrency.

In this context, Philion said that XRP holders need to consider three main questions.

The first question is what XRP holders are actually doing to utilize their XRP holdings. The second is how they can convince potential investors to hold XRP.

Finally, the third point that the Flare CEO mentioned is that the XRP community should find a way to transform XRP from a predominantly speculative asset into usable capital.

"And that's part of the reason we created Flare because we feel like there's always been more that can and should be done with XRP," the Flare CEO added.

Besides, the Flare CEO also invited XRP holders to try Flare. According to Philion, it's a way to make XRP less dependent on Ripple.

The Flare CEO's statements come amid increasing frustration around XRP and the lackluster performance of the cryptocurrency.

Over the past year, the price of XRP has gone down by around 50%, and at press time, the price of the cryptocurrency stands at $1.16.

Recently, Ripple's former chief technology officer David Schwartz also faced some criticism over the poor performance of XRP. Schwartz noted that his influence on the XRP price was very limited.

One X user raised a question about whether FLR holders are doing enough to demonstrate the value of the cryptocurrency, especially when it's possible for XRP holders to use Flare's decentralized finance ecosystem without purchasing FLR.

Still, Philion dismissed the claim that there is no need for FLR when using FXRP (a tokenized version of XRP on Flare).

"It's incorrect btw that you can use FXRP without needing FLR," the Flare CEO clarified.

According to Philion, what Flare Smart Accounts do is simply abstract away the need to manage FLR and make the experience more convenient. At the same time, FLR is used to pay transaction fees on the underlying networks.

Besides, the Flare CEO also underlined that in the long term, there should be more use cases for the token apart from transaction fees.

"Usage is important. Need to get fee generation going in a broader way in the ecosystem," he added.

#5132. OpenAI Found 948 Linux Security Flaws—And Figured Out How to Exploit Them

OpenAI Found 948 Linux Security Flaws—And Figured Out How to Exploit Them

OpenAI says its autonomous security agents discovered 948 distinct vulnerabilities in the Linux kernel, developed working exploits and drafted fixes for review—all for roughly $1.2 million in API credits.

“We chose Linux to study how effectively frontier models could find vulnerabilities and demonstrate their security impact in a codebase of this scale and importance,” OpenAI said in results published Wednesday.

The Linux kernel powers cloud servers, Android devices and much of the world's critical computing infrastructure. The flaws identified in OpenAI's experiment were local privilege-escalation vulnerabilities: An attacker would first need limited access to a vulnerable machine, but could then potentially obtain root privileges and take full control.

OpenAI's agents scanned about 2.8 million lines of kernel code, or roughly 7% of the project. “Our prototype identified and analyzed 2,308 candidate Linux kernel findings, dynamically reproduced 1,724 vulnerabilities, and produced 948 local privilege escalation (LPE) exploits that could allow an unprivileged local user to gain root access” OpenAI said.

After grouping exploits by their underlying cause and proposed fix, researchers counted 948 distinct flaws.

The October run cost $1,213,430 in API credits, equivalent to around $1,279 per vulnerability. OpenAI compared that with historical $20,000-to-$70,000 payouts for Linux privilege-escalation exploits at hacking competitions, suggesting a hypothetical value of $18.9 million to $66.3 million if the same results were found manually.

An earlier GPT-5.6 Sol campaign found 166 unique privilege-escalation vulnerabilities in July for $513,690. A newer, unreleased cyber model found more than five times as many in October, even after the kernel had undergone months of fixes.

OpenAI also said its agents used at least 10 exploitation techniques unfamiliar to the researchers and outside experts they consulted—not merely existing tricks retrieved from training data.

AI companies finding critical bugs are just another proof of how systems are capable of performing advanced cybersecurity research achieving results that previously required significant human expertise.

Last week, Anthropic disclosed that an unreleased version of Claude had discovered and exploited serious vulnerabilities in widely used software, including Chrome and Linux. Some of those exploits could execute malicious code remotely with little or no user interaction.

The economics of this research were already changing before OpenAI's announcement.

Researchers at Vidoc Security reproduced several vulnerability discoveries associated with Anthropic's powerful Mythos AI using publicly accessible models for less than $30 per file.

Those tests showed that finding vulnerabilities was becoming increasingly accessible, although reproducing a flaw is different from developing a reliable exploit capable of compromising a protected system.

OpenAI's latest experiment goes further by automating much of that exploitation process, creating a potential advantage for defenders trying to patch software before criminals can take advantage of the same weaknesses.

The company warned that attackers with limited access to an outdated operating system may increasingly be able to escalate their privileges with AI assistance.

OpenAI is attempting to restrict access to its more capable cybersecurity models through verified security programs. Its Daybreak Red initiative provides approved defenders with access to specialized AI tools for vulnerability research, exploit testing, and mitigation development.

#5131. Zcash Upgrade Could Lock $16 Million in Crypto Forever

Zcash Upgrade Could Lock $16 Million in Crypto Forever

At recent market prices, those holdings represent roughly $15 million to $16 million that could become effectively frozen when the network activates its NU7 upgrade.

“NU7 (block 3,543,405) makes v4 transactions invalid, so what remains in Sprout is unspendable: frozen, not burned,” Zecstats says in its information card for Sprout.

The upgrade is scheduled for block 3,543,405, expected Thursday evening in the United States. The exact deadline depends on how quickly the blockchain processes new blocks, rather than a predetermined clock time.

Unlike Bitcoin, where transactions are generally visible to anyone examining the blockchain, Zcash allows users to conceal transaction amounts and the addresses involved. It accomplishes this through technology called zero-knowledge proofs, which can verify that a transaction is valid without revealing its private details.

NU7 changes that by eliminating version-4 transactions, the only format capable of moving coins out of Sprout. According to the network's technical specification, transactions using that format will become invalid after activation.

The decision addresses a problem familiar to anyone maintaining old software: supporting obsolete technology creates additional complexity and potential security vulnerabilities.

Developers haven't technically destroyed the coins. They will continue counting toward Zcash's circulating issuance calculations, and a future network upgrade could theoretically restore access, but that is not guaranteed, and the team has not shown any interest in doing so.

In fact, the specification explicitly warns that spending those funds may never be re-enabled.

Legacy holders are the people most exposed to the change. Anyone whose ZEC remains in Sprout must move it through a compatible withdrawal mechanism before the upgrade activates, or risk losing access indefinitely.

Sprout addresses begin with "zc," which can help users recognize old wallet records. Regular ZEC holders using modern supported wallets do not need to migrate their coins out of Sprout, although keeping wallet software updated remains important.

The blockchain also cannot reveal who owns the remaining Sprout balance, meaning nobody knows how much belongs to active users and how much is already inaccessible because its owners lost their keys.

Meanwhile, NU7 brings a benefit for everyone else: Zcash's target block interval falls from 75 seconds to 25 seconds, meaning transactions should receive their first confirmation approximately three times faster, according to the Zcash Foundation.

For Sprout holders, however, the relevant number is block 3,543,405 which will happen later today. As of the time of publication, the latest available block is 3,543,027. Once the network reaches block 3,543,405, any remaining coins in the old pool will be frozen under the new rules.

#5130. GOPAX halts crypto trading, withdrawals to move all assets to cold storage

South Korea’s GOPAX suspended trading, deposits and standard withdrawals after a hacking threat, with manual withdrawals available through support.

South Korean crypto exchange GOPAX suspended trading, deposits and standard withdrawals Friday after receiving an email threatening a hack, according to local newspaper Kyunghyang Shinmun.

A GOPAX representative told the newspaper that no hack or attempted theft had occurred at that point.

GOPAX said it halted deposits and standard withdrawals at 5:55 am UTC. Trading stopped at 6:53 UTC, according to a second announcement. All unfilled orders were canceled.

GOPAX said it plans to transfer all crypto assets to offline wallets to protect users following a series of leaks and hacking incidents affecting financial institutions. Its notices cited financial regulators’ recommendations.

Cointelegraph reached out to GOPAX for comment but had not received a response by publication time.

Customers can still request withdrawals through GOPAX’s support center, but the exchange warned that manual verification and approvals could delay processing.

The suspension came days after South Korea’s Financial Services Commission (FSC) urged domestic exchanges to increase cold-wallet holdings following the $6.3 billion Binance hack. South Korean exchanges must keep at least 80% of customer crypto assets in cold storage.

Financial Supervisory Service data previously showed that GOPAX held 80.54% of user assets offline at the end of September, near the required minimum.

In October, the FSC reported cybersecurity deficiencies at 130 of 555 financial companies reviewed nationwide. The regulator also identified GOPAX operator Streami and crypto exchange Prabang among 16 companies involved in earlier security incidents, prompting plans for tighter cybersecurity oversight.

South Korean exchange Prabang also suspended services after detecting abnormal withdrawals from its hot wallet on Oct. 24. The exchange reported about 100 million won ($70,000) in losses, according to Yonhap News Agency. Prabang said it would reimburse affected customers using company funds.

#5129. A single AI forecast inflates US power auctions by $5.7B

AI demand is pushing up computer costs and straining electricity markets, and the spending boom is creating new inflation pressures.

Australia's latest inflation figures showed that prices in the category covering audio, visual, and computing equipment rose 6.1% in a single quarter.

That's by far the largest increase in the Australian Bureau of Statistics series, which began in 1980. Computers, TVs, and electronics in general have gotten cheaper and delivered more performance for the same money.

Customers around the world, including Australia, have (reasonably) expected this trend to continue.

This is why the third quarter came as such a surprise, as it interrupted a consumer expectation that has been in place for decades.

The Reserve Bank of Australia linked higher prices for some computers and other consumer electronics to rising memory-chip costs associated with demand for AI infrastructure.

The same components, or the manufacturing capacity needed to produce them, are now being pursued by companies whose shopping budgets look nothing like yours.

On Nov. 3, the RBA raised its cash rate to 4.85%, citing AI-related investment as one of several sources of price pressure. Higher fuel costs and domestic capacity constraints were also involved, and the bank said AI's effect on overall consumer prices remained modest.

The technology hasn't single-handedly taken over Australian monetary policy, although it has progressed from a promise in investor presentations to a consideration in decisions that affect ordinary borrowers.

We've also seen this in the US, where AI demand has reached much further into the system that determines the cost of electricity. PJM, the grid operator serving all or parts of 13 states and Washington, D.C., runs auctions that pay power plants and other resources to be available when the grid needs them, years before the electricity is actually used.

In its analysis of this summer's auction, PJM’s independent market monitor calculated that power providers would receive about $16.4 billion for making capacity available during the 2028-29 delivery year.

The monitor then reran the auction without the forecast growth in demand from new and expanding data centers, while leaving existing data-center demand in place.

Under that scenario, the total fell to roughly $10.7 billion, down $5.7 billion. The calculation shows how expectations of future data-center demand can affect the price of keeping power available.

Together, the Australian prices and the American auction show how big AI's effect on inflation is: the spending starts producing identifiable, quantifiable consequences long before we see any productivity gains.

The costs also reach people who aren't buying AI services, while the institutions assigning those costs are still working out how much responsibility the companies requesting the infrastructure should carry.

Data centers are buildings packed with computing equipment, along with the cooling systems and electricity connections needed to keep it working.

However, AI demand extends well beyond the GPUs that attract most of the attention.

Filling a data center with expensive chips also requires the hardware that feeds them information and keeps them running, creating competition for parts and factory capacity that other electronics manufacturers depend on.

The RBA's account follows that competition through to Australian shops, where higher memory costs contributed to more expensive consumer electronics.

The ABS's 6.1% quarterly equipment-price increase covers a particular statistical category, so it can't be treated as the price increase for every computer, much less a precise measurement of AI's contribution. It does, however, give the central bank's explanation an observable consumer outcome.

Again, something similar has been happening in the US. The September consumer-price report recorded a 9.7% annual increase for computers, peripherals, and smart-home assistants, while smartphone prices were 9.9% lower than a year earlier.

CPI-U price changes from September 2025 to September 2026, not seasonally adjusted. The all-items index provides a benchmark for the two product categories. These movements do not isolate AI's contribution to prices.

Source: BLS, September 2026 CPI release, Table 2

But even though we've seen large increases in the affected categories, they've actually had a relatively small effect on the average household's entire spending basket.

The San Francisco Fed calculated that prices for information-processing equipment rose 13% over the year to August, but added only 0.2 percentage points to that month's 3.4% headline inflation rate under the personal consumption expenditures measure. Its economists found limited evidence that those pressures had spread broadly across the economy, where energy costs and tariff-related goods prices were doing considerably more of the damage.

The Bank of England was also monitoring demand for AI components as a source of global cost pressure, but judged the potential effect of these and other non-energy risks on UK inflation to be much smaller than the energy shock and little changed since September.

Its quarter-point increase in Bank Rate to 4% was justified by energy-driven inflation risks, alongside stronger-than-expected activity.

Another complication in software is that a subscription can become more expensive when it starts doing something its previous version couldn't.

Federal Reserve researchers have warned that parts of the inflation data may not adequately account for those improvements. Paying more can still hurt a household budget, even when part of the increase reflects better quality.

Electricity is another way costs can spread because both new and existing customers depend on the same system. Someone replacing a laptop can postpone the purchase, whereas households connected to a grid that accommodates substantial new demand have far less control over what gets built and how the resulting costs reach them.

PJM coordinates the power system across much of the eastern US, and one of its jobs is to make sure enough electricity can be supplied when demand is high.

Alongside markets for delivered electricity, it runs auctions that pay suppliers to commit capacity for a future year, meaning they agree to be available under the market's rules when the system needs them.

That availability has a separate price from the electricity eventually generated. Power stations need sufficient revenue to remain in service or justify investment, while the operator has to plan before households and businesses switch on equipment that will require supply.

Forecasting how much power customers will need therefore becomes part of a financial transaction.

PJM's July auction for the 2028-29 delivery year produced approximately $16.41 billion in auction revenue. In a recent analysis, its independent market monitor, Monitoring Analytics, reran the auction without the forecast growth in demand from new data centers and expansions of existing facilities, while leaving other assumptions unchanged.

The resulting revenue fell to approximately $10.7 billion, a modeled difference of $5.7 billion for that delivery year.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Revenue for the 2028–29 capacity delivery year. The counterfactual removes forecast growth from new and expanding data centers, retains existing load, and holds other assumptions fixed. The gap is a modeled capacity-market effect, not a household surcharge or an estimate of AI alone.

Source: Monitoring Analytics, October 30, 2026, Table 11, scenarios 0 and 3

The calculation shows how the forecast growth affects revenue inside the monitor's model, using the auction's rules and its existing assumptions about available supply.

While these numbers are only an estimate of generative AI demand, the financial exposure is still substantial, and it starts with demand expectations rather than requiring every proposed building to be operating.

Planning too little capacity creates an obvious risk of shortages, while planning for projects that consume much less power than expected can leave expensive infrastructure with fewer customers than its financing assumed.

Those decisions reach households through utilities and electricity suppliers, with the eventual effect depending on contracts and local regulation.

Capacity payments are one part of power costs, so an increase in that market can't simply be relabeled as the same percentage increase in a residential bill. Customers whose suppliers have already secured power under contracts may see a different result from customers more directly exposed to new market prices.

Virginia regulators have established a separate rate class for Dominion Energy Virginia's large users and requirements intended to make them cover more of the costs associated with serving them, including minimum payments and contractual safeguards.

The requirement to take and pay for electricity for at least 14 years applies to new large-load customers contracting for service on or after Jan. 1, 2027.

Those eligibility rules determine which projects the safeguards cover and whether other customers remain exposed if a developer reserves substantial capacity and subsequently needs less of it.

Tech companies can pay their own invoices and still affect prices elsewhere, because increased demand can require more expensive supply or new shared infrastructure.

Equally, a large customer making reliable long-term payments can help spread existing costs across more consumption, which is why the evidence doesn't support treating every data center as an automatic surcharge on the neighborhood.

The MIT working paper revised in October estimated that data-center entry raised residential electricity rates by 2.1%, with larger effects among investor-owned utilities, using data from 2010 through 2024.

Researchers behind another working paper, using a different method and covering 2015 through 2024, estimated a modest average reduction in retail rates and warned that future supply constraints could reverse that effect.

However, both studies analyzed different units and used different methods to identify the effect. Also, both studies largely precede the current scale of AI expansion and data center construction.

Their disagreement on the data is a good reason to look at local systems and the rules governing them, rather than choosing whichever national average makes the most satisfying claim.

Someone using AI to complete a task faster has experienced a real benefit, but turning that saved time into lower prices across an economy will take a lot more time.

Businesses have to organize work around the technology, produce more with the resources they have, and pass some of the resulting savings to customers rather than retaining all of them.

We are yet to see that transition happen. A survey of nearly 6,000 executives across four countries found that 89% reported no effect on their firm's productivity over the previous three years, while other Fed-linked research found evidence consistent with productivity gains among adopters.

In its Nov. 5 preliminary release, the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported that nonfarm business labor productivity rose 3.6% in the third quarter, while unit labor costs fell 0.7%, both at seasonally adjusted annualized rates. Businesses produced more per hour worked, with labor compensation per unit of output declining.

However, the release doesn't identify how much AI contributed to those gains or establish how much of the savings reached customers.

Nonfarm business sector, percent change from the previous quarter at a seasonally adjusted annual rate. Figures use the November 5, 2026 release; Q3 2026 is preliminary. The series measure output per hour and compensation per unit of output, without identifying AI's contribution or savings passed to consumers.

Source: BLS, Productivity and Costs, November 5, 2026, Table 2

Even widespread productivity improvements wouldn't automatically produce an equally large fall in inflation.

Fed Governor Lisa Cook explained in September that expectations of higher future incomes and returns can encourage more spending today, adding demand alongside the technology's eventual increase in supply.

Companies are already investing in anticipation of those gains, while households holding appreciated assets may also be more willing to spend.

The RBA expects data-center investment to support business activity, but estimates that roughly 70% of the additional investment will be imported, limiting the direct boost to domestic production even as local construction requires labor and materials.

Its November decision addressed a broader inflation problem, with AI contributing to particular pressures in an economy already facing plenty of others.

The companies building AI are making private bets on future demand, while regulators and utilities must decide how much of the infrastructure required to meet those bets other customers should pay for.

While greater productivity could eventually benefit households and businesses far beyond the technology industry, those future gains don't settle who should pay for grid expansion, or who pays if a project requests more power than it needs.

#5128. Argentine Bitcoiners Debut “The Genesis of Satoshi Nakamoto” Artwork in Downtown Buenos Aires

“The Genesis of Satoshi Nakamoto,” a 2.4-meter, multi-dimensional cube, was presented on October 29 at Polo Dot as part of the Open Fest of LABITCONF 2026. Driven by Bitcoin.ar through the Premio B·Arte, the work combines art, technology, and education in a journey through Bitcoin’s fundamental principles. The inauguration featured guided remarks by Rodolfo Andragnes and a musical performance by Mayumaná, according to an announcement by the organization.

Buenos Aires now has a monument dedicated to Satoshi Nakamoto, the person—or group of people—behind the creation of Bitcoin, whose identity remains unknown.

Driven by Bitcoin.ar through the fifth edition of the Premio B·Arte, the monument was conceived not only as a tribute to Satoshi, but also as an artistic and educational experience. Through its five faces—four lateral and one upper—it proposes a journey to approach different concepts that explain Bitcoin’s origin, functioning, and philosophy.

During the inauguration, Rodolfo Andragnes — co-founder of Bitcoin.ar and LABITCONF — accompanied the public with words and explanations about the details of the work, guiding the reading of its various elements and the meaning behind each representation. The presentation was accompanied by a musical show by Mayumaná, which added rhythm and sound to the opening of this new space dedicated to Bitcoin’s history.

Each of the monument’s faces offers a gateway to a different aspect of Bitcoin. The figure of Satoshi Nakamoto, for example, represents the anonymous character of its creator and the collective dimension of a technology that, once set in motion, continued to grow thanks to thousands of people distributed around the world. The reflections and multiplied geometric forms also evoke the structure of a network that replicates and expands.

Another of its faces addresses Bitcoin’s programmed scarcity through a cube that successively divides into halves in a Fibonacci sequence. The representation refers to “the halving”, the mechanism that approximately every four years halves the reward miners receive for validating blocks and that regulates the issuance of new bitcoins. Starting at 50 bitcoins per block, the total reward gets cut in half every four years for roughly 130 years, until 21 million bitcoins have been emitted and the block reward becomes zero. This supply limit is iconic to the cryptocurrency and key to its qualities as hard money.

The work also incorporates the first words of Bitcoin’s foundational document, the white paper published by Satoshi Nakamoto in October 31st,  2008. There, the possibility of making electronic payments directly between people, without needing to go through a financial institution, is proposed. That idea constitutes a foundational principle of the technology.

Another face reproduces information from the genesis block, the first block of the Bitcoin chain, created on January 3, 2009. Among its elements appears the reference to the headline from The Times newspaper that Satoshi incorporated into that block, a historical mark that places Bitcoin’s birth in its context and anchors it to history and time.

Thus, the monument proposes something more than contemplating a sculpture: it invites one to walk around it, observe it from different angles, and discover the concepts behind each of its parts; “The intention is to bring Bitcoin closer to those who are already part of its community, but also to awaken the curiosity of those who do not yet know its history.”

The monument was created by Ricardo Alonso, engineer, and Celeste Difabio Videla, architect, a couple of artists from Bariloche who won the fifth edition of the Premio B·Arte. The work measures 2.40 meters in width, length, and height, and is made of stainless steel and glass.

Its construction involved teams and collaborators from different localities across the country, in a process that combined different trades, materials, and techniques. Aída Pippo, visual artist, researcher of new technologies applied to art, and member of the Board of Directors of Bitcoin.ar, led the project and coordinated the different stages of production and assembly.

“It gives me great pride to feel that this is something we can leave in our city and in our country,” Andragnes expressed during the presentation, highlighting the value of a collective initiative that seeks to leave a cultural and educational footprint.

For their part, the creators noted that they sought for the work to function as a meeting point for the community and, at the same time, as a gateway for those who do not yet know Bitcoin. “You don’t always have the possibility of leaving something for posterity,” they shared when speaking of the experience of seeing the monument finished and installed in Buenos Aires.

With this inauguration, Bitcoin.ar seeks for the monument to become a permanent resource for the city: a space that schools, universities, families, and visitors can approach to learn Bitcoin’s history, discover its principles, and open new conversations about the future of money and technology. The intention is that what is presented today as an artistic work can continue to grow through educational activities and new experiences, and remain in Buenos Aires as a legacy for future generations.

The project was financed by the Fundación Bitcoin Iberoamérica and represents a new way of bringing Bitcoin’s history and principles to public and cultural spaces, beyond screens and specialized environments.

#5126. Nvidia’s CEO, an alleged romance and a suspected Chinese spy: Congress wants answers

The reported intelligence memo adds detail to an earlier House briefing request, with an answer sought by November 11.

Congress is seeking answers about Nvidia’s defenses against espionage as Financial Times reporting describes an intelligence-based memo alleging a romantic relationship between CEO Jensen Huang and Alice Huang, an executive at Singapore-based Nvidia customer Megaspeed.

FT excerpts shared by @AsiaLens on November 7 report that US intelligence believes Alice Huang was recruited by China’s Ministry of State Security, citing people familiar with the matter.

Three House committee chairs signed a November 4 letter requesting a joint, closed-door briefing on Huang’s interactions with Alice Huang and Nvidia’s safeguards against foreign espionage. Published on November 5, the request predates the FT excerpts.

Nvidia has described the contacts as ordinary customer business and said it does not share classified information. The central security question is whether the alleged relationship provided access beyond ordinary customer contact.

The FT excerpts shared by @AsiaLens say US officials wrote the memo earlier in 2026 using information obtained by American intelligence. People familiar with the memo said it alleged Huang had been romantically involved with Alice Huang, an executive at Megaspeed, a Singapore-based Nvidia customer.

According to the same excerpts, the memo reached a small group of senior US officials shortly before President Donald Trump traveled to Beijing for a summit with Xi Jinping. The memo itself is not public, and its exact date and authoring agency are not established. The reported assessment and relationship allegation have not been publicly proven.

The letter was signed by Brian Mast, John Moolenaar and Rick Crawford, who chair the Foreign Affairs, China Select and Intelligence committees, respectively. Their public release identifies possible foreign-adversary access to US technology and information as the reason for seeking the briefing.

A November 4 letter preceded the FT excerpts shared on November 7. Explore the lawmakers’ request and Nvidia’s stated limits on customer access.

#5125. Mercado Bitcoin Said Its Hack Was Contained, But Blockchain Data Tells a Different Story

Mercado Bitcoin Said Its Hack Was Contained, But Blockchain Data Tells a Different Story

An attacker drained Mercado Bitcoin-linked deposit addresses nearly four hours after the Brazilian exchange said it had contained a security breach, new blockchain research shows.

Bitquery's analysis, updated Thursday, traces more than $48 million in stolen assets across 11 blockchains. Its findings challenge the exchange's claim that it stopped the attack quickly.

The Bitcoin exchange announced at 9:56 a.m. Brazil time on November 2 that it had quickly contained the incident. Bitquery says 36,042 Ethereum customer deposit addresses were emptied between 9:27 a.m. and 1:56 p.m. Brazil time.

Those addresses held roughly $266,000, a small fraction of the total theft. But the attacker could still move those funds after the exchange’s announcement.

A customer deposit address works much like a personal receiving account for cryptocurrency. The exchange later gathers those coins into a shared online wallet, called a hot wallet, to process everyday transactions. Moving funds from a deposit address requires its private key, the secret that authorizes transfers.

Bitquery says the withdrawals were authorized directly by the deposit addresses, suggesting the attacker had access to their private keys or to a system capable of signing transactions on their behalf.

“That needs a key for each address, or a way into the system that signs for them,” the researchers explained. They warned that exchanges typically generate deposit keys from a single master key, meaning the attacker may still be able to access other customer addresses.

The amount stolen is also disputed. Initial estimates put the amount above $40 million, while Mercado Bitcoin's updated FAQ now puts affected assets at about $36.3 million. Bitquery estimates that $48.6 million was stolen across 11 blockchains.

Mercado Bitcoin's public updates have not explained the gap between its containment claim and the later transfers. The company assured customers it would cover user losses with its own funds.

Bitquery warns customers against reusing old deposit addresses until the exchange confirms they are safe. Mercado Bitcoin's reopening plan begins November 9 with balance viewing only; deposits, withdrawals and trading have no restart date.

#5123. South Korean Crypto Exchange Gopax Suspends Withdrawals and Trading After Hacking Threat

South Korea's Gopax halted standard withdrawals at 2:55 p.m. and trading at 3:53 p.m. Friday after a hacking-threat email, and is transferring all held assets to cold wallets with no loss reported so far.

South Korean cryptocurrency exchange Gopax suspended withdrawals and trading on Friday after receiving a hacking-threat email, and said it is moving all held virtual assets into offline cold wallets as a preemptive step to protect customer funds.

Gopax halted standard deposit and withdrawal services at 2:55 p.m. local time and stopped trading at 3:53 p.m., Yonhap News Agency reported, citing the exchange. Direct withdrawals through the website and app are unavailable. Customers who want coins out must apply through the customer support center, where requests are processed manually after internal verification.

“To ensure the safe custody of user assets, we plan to transfer all virtual assets to cold wallets for storage,” Gopax said in remarks carried by Seoul Economic Daily. “Direct withdrawals through the Gopax website or app are temporarily unavailable, and customers wishing to withdraw funds should apply through the Gopax customer support center.”

No actual loss of assets has been reported. The company has not set a date to reopen trading or standard withdrawals.

With trading offline, all open orders were canceled, Chosun Biz reported. Deposits were initially paused with withdrawals; later Korean coverage said deposits had been restored while web and app withdrawals stayed shut. Manual withdrawals through support will take longer than usual.

A cold wallet is a storage setup kept offline, cut off from the internet that hot wallets use to process customer sends. Gopax said the shift of remaining hot-wallet balances into cold storage is meant to shrink the surface available to anyone who can reach online systems.

A Gopax official told Yonhap the email came from a party claiming it could hack the exchange, but that the threat itself had not been verified. “We ask for your understanding, as this measure is intended to prevent financial incidents and protect user assets,” the company said.

The freeze lands in a week when exchange security is already under a bright light. On Oct. 29, attackers drained about $6.34 billion from Binance hot wallets in what Binance later described as a months-long campaign that began with a fake job interview. The FBI has tied that theft to North Korea; Binance has reopened withdrawals in stages and said it is covering customer losses from its own funds.

Brazil’s Mercado Bitcoin froze deposits and withdrawals earlier this week after its own security incident, with preliminary estimates of suspicious transfers near $40 million. Gopax’s case is different on the facts disclosed so far: the exchange is describing a threat email and a precautionary lockdown, not a confirmed drain.

Gopax itself has been dealing with account trouble. From early September, abnormal access hit some user accounts, leaking personal information on seven customers and two current or former employees, with further leakage confirmed last month, Yonhap’s Korean dispatch said. The company has treated that breach as separate from Friday’s threat email; any link has not been confirmed.

South Korea’s Financial Services Commission has also been pressing domestic platforms after a broader wave of cybersecurity incidents across financial firms. Korean exchanges are already required to keep at least 80% of customer crypto in cold storage. Friday’s move pushes Gopax further offline while it checks whether the threat is real.

For bitcoin holders on Gopax, the practical effect is a queue: coins are being parked offline while support staff approve outbound sends one by one. That is slower than an app button, and it is the trade the exchange says it is making to keep balances intact.

The test for Gopax now is whether it can finish the cold-wallet transfer, clear the manual withdrawal queue without a loss, and say when trading comes back — or whether the unverified threat turns into the incident it was trying to get ahead of.

#5122. One BlackRock fund dominates Bitcoin’s billion-dollar ETF exodus

Fidelity finished positive in the November 2-6 week, but the other funds collectively remained negative without IBIT.

BlackRock's iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF (IBIT) accounted for 94% of US spot Bitcoin ETFs' net withdrawals in the completed November 2-6 week, leaving one fund responsible for most of the category's retreat. That leaves the next recovery test focused on one fund: can IBIT’s withdrawals slow?

The 12 funds tracked by Farside Investors recorded weekly net outflows of $1,282 million. IBIT recorded net outflows of $1,210 million. Fidelity's Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC) moved in the opposite direction, recording weekly net inflows of $93 million.

IBIT's share was 94.4% of the aggregate net withdrawal, after positive flows elsewhere offset part of the withdrawals. The denominator is net fund flows across the group, not gross redemptions or all Bitcoin selling.

All funds other than IBIT collectively recorded net outflows of $72 million during the week. Removing IBIT's withdrawals from that calculation would substantially reduce the shortfall while leaving the remaining group negative.

The daily pattern also matters. IBIT recorded withdrawals in each of the five sessions, while the group finished positive only on November 4. US spot Bitcoin ETFs recorded net outflows of $412 million on November 6.

Fidelity was a counterweight across the week, despite recording withdrawals on Thursday. Three other funds also finished the week positive. The picture was therefore more uneven than a universal exit from Bitcoin funds, although the positive products did not offset the category's largest withdrawal source.

Wednesday's Fidelity-led inflows failed to persist: Thursday's withdrawals restored the negative aggregate reading, and Friday ended the week with another outflow.

iShares reported IBIT net assets of about $61 billion as of November 6. The week's withdrawals were roughly 2% of that ending asset base.

Redemptions also need care when connecting fund flows to Bitcoin selling. The IBIT prospectus describes both cash and in-kind settlement. A cash redemption involves the trust selling bitcoin and delivering cash. An in-kind redemption delivers bitcoin to an authorized participant, the intermediary handling the redemption, or its designated agent or client.

For ETF demand, the distinction is observable. Smaller IBIT withdrawals alongside a negative aggregate total would show easing redemption pressure at IBIT. Positive aggregate flows would establish net ETF inflows for that session; participation across funds and persistence over subsequent completed sessions would provide stronger evidence of sustained fund demand.

#5121. Greece approves 10% tax on crypto gains starting in 2027

Greece has approved a 10% tax on cryptocurrency gains starting in 2027, with an exemption for investors whose annual net gains do not exceed 500 euros.

Greece’s parliament approved legislation imposing a 10% tax on cryptocurrency gains, establishing new rules for investors as the country prepares to introduce the levy in 2027.

The Hellenic Parliament passed the tax measures Thursday as part of a broader bill covering loan oversight, debt protections and financial market regulation.

The bill introduces a 10% tax on profits from crypto asset sales but exempts investors whose annual gains do not exceed 500 euros ($560), local financial publication Oikonomikos Tachydromos reported Friday. The new framework is scheduled to take effect on Jan. 1, 2027.

The legislation also imposes a 15% tax on income from staking, crypto lending and providing liquidity to decentralized protocols. Using crypto to buy goods or services may also trigger capital gains tax, while swapping one crypto asset for another does not generate taxable gains, the report notes.

Greece’s Ministry of National Economy and Finance first proposed the tax in October. The legislation gives investors 12 months to voluntarily declare previously unreported crypto gains. They must pay the resulting tax within 60 days of filing to avoid penalties and interest.

#5119. DOJ Creates 'Super Intelligence' Task Force to Guard Against AI Abuse

DOJ Creates 'Super Intelligence' Task Force to Guard Against AI Abuse

The U.S. Department of Justice is setting up a task force dedicated to policing the misuse of advanced artificial intelligence, as Washington races to build enforcement muscle around a technology it increasingly frames in near-sci-fi terms.

Announced Thursday, the Super Intelligence Task Force, or DOJ-SITF, will be led by Associate Attorney General Stanley Woodward and Associate Deputy Attorney General Stephen Weiss.

Its stated mission is to make sure American leadership in “super intelligence” isn’t compromised by bad actors, whether foreign governments and their proxies, transnational criminal organizations, or companies and individuals using AI to commit fraud or other crimes.

The department invoked President Donald Trump’s framing that “America stands at the forefront of a new technological revolution in intelligence.”

“SI development will continue to be powered by American innovators,” Attorney General Todd Blanche said in a statement, adding that the task force would work alongside SI Force Chair Jay Clayton to keep critical AI technologies from being “manipulated by bad actors at home or abroad.”

Blanche said the DOJ would keep “identifying and prosecuting individuals whose use of SI violates the law, including by maliciously targeting American taxpayers, critical U.S. infrastructure, or other aspects of our national security.”

Notably, the effort isn’t purely about catching criminals. Weiss said the department also has “a responsibility” to “harden regular reporting on SI risk and response, and ensure companies exercise vigorous internal controls so that SI technology behaves as intended,” signaling that the DOJ wants AI developers themselves to tighten safeguards against models going off the rails.

The term “super intelligence” typically refers to a hypothetical AI that surpasses human capability across the board, and the government’s embrace of the phrase marks an escalation in how officials talk about the technology. Former SEC chair Jay Clayton has been tied to a broader Trump administration push around super intelligence, and the SEC recently added “super intelligence” to its 2027 examination priorities.

The task force joins a widening federal scramble to get ahead of AI risk. Trump signed an executive order setting up a fight with states over AI regulation. Earlier this week, the U.S. banking regulator OCC issued its first AI framework as banks battle rogue agents reaching into customer accounts.

The DOJ did not specify a timeline or detail how the task force would coordinate with other agencies, saying only that its work would “promote innovation and prevent the misuse” of the technology.

#5117. The diesel problem that could spoil Bitcoin’s hopes for cheaper money

National inventories fell to 99.26 million barrels, while lower weekly diesel and heating-oil prices kept the financing risk conditional.

U.S. inventories of distillate fuel oil, which includes diesel and heating oil, fell below 100 million barrels in the week ended Oct. 30, even as the latest surveyed fuel prices declined. The smaller stock buffer leaves fuel costs a potential obstacle to inflation relief and easier financing for Bitcoin investors.

The U.S. Energy Information Administration's Nov. 4 report showed stocks of 99.26 million barrels, down from 102.82 million a week earlier. That 3.57-million-barrel decline left inventories 13% below their five-year average.

U.S. on-highway diesel averaged $6.29 a gallon on Nov. 2, down 6 cents in a week. EIA's surveyed residential heating-oil average fell 9.2 cents to $6.14 a gallon over the same period. Diesel prices include taxes; the heating-oil series excludes them.

Both fuels remained substantially more expensive than a year earlier: diesel was up $2.54 a gallon and residential heating oil was up $2.51.

There is also continuing refinery supply. Utilization increased to 94.5% from 92.8% in the week ended Oct. 30, although distillate production edged down to 5.154 million barrels a day from 5.161 million. Higher overall refinery activity therefore coexisted with a further draw on distillate stocks.

National distillate stocks fell below 100 million barrels. The latest surveyed diesel and heating-oil prices fell weekly, while staying higher than a year earlier.

Nov. 2, 2026: both surveyed fuel prices fell from the preceding week. Their small declines sit near zero on the common scale.

Nov. 2, 2026: the same prices remained much higher than a year earlier. Weekly relief does not erase elevated fuel costs.

A smaller stock buffer can coexist with falling weekly prices; Bitcoin's financing risk remains conditional.

National stocks and surveyed fuel prices have different observation dates. Diesel includes taxes; residential heating oil excludes them. The 100-million mark is not an exhaustion boundary. Neither view measures inflation pass-through, Fed repricing or a Bitcoin reaction.

Stock positions use a labelled linear 95–105 million-barrel scale. Fuel-price change positions use a common linear -$3 to +$3 per-gallon scale in both views. Prices and changes are EIA reported figures; no inflation or Bitcoin response is calculated.

National stocks and surveyed fuel prices have different observation dates. Diesel includes taxes; residential heating oil excludes them. The 100-million mark is not an exhaustion boundary. Neither view measures inflation pass-through, Fed repricing or a Bitcoin reaction.

Diesel powers freight, while heating oil adds directly to household bills. Persistent fuel costs can make moving goods and heating homes more expensive. If that pressure slows broader inflation relief, it could obstruct easier financing conditions for Bitcoin investors.

Historical research explains why that possibility matters. Authors of the August 2023 International Monetary Fund working paper The Crypto Cycle and US Monetary Policy found that Fed tightening reduced a common crypto-price measure through investors' willingness to take risk.

The Fed held its target range at 3.75% to 4% on Oct. 28 and said inflation remained elevated. That decision preceded the inventory release.

EIA's next weekly petroleum report is scheduled for Nov. 12 at noon Eastern, covering the week ended Nov. 6. Sustained inventory rebuilding alongside lower fuel costs would weaken the inflation concern. Continued draws would keep attention on the stock buffer, but their significance for Bitcoin would depend on whether fuel pressure reaches broader inflation and financing conditions.

#5116. Bitcoin Price Holds Near $75,000 After Hot October Jobs Report

U.S. employers added 165,000 jobs in October, nearly three times the 59,000 economists expected, the Labor Department said Friday, and the bitcoin price briefly pushed above $76,100 before settling near $75,500, Bitcoin Magazine Pro data shows.

The unemployment rate edged down to 4.1% from 4.2%, beating the Dow Jones forecast that called for it to hold. Average hourly earnings rose 0.4% on the month and 3.1% over the year, both ahead of estimates, CNBC reported.

Prior months were revised higher as well. August payrolls moved to 186,000, an upward revision of 53,000, and September rose to 60,000 after a 31,000 upgrade, leaving the two-month total 84,000 stronger than first reported.

The print lands on a market still digesting Thursday’s Nasdaq-led rout. The bitcoin price had held near $76,000 into that close, dipped toward $75,200 late in the session, then recovered into the mid-$75,000s overnight before the 8:30 a.m. ET release. Spot bitcoin changed hands around $75,800 after the data, with a market cap of about $1.52 trillion and roughly $48 billion in trading volume over the past day, Yahoo Finance data indicated.

Bitcoin price’s first move was a quick spike above $76,100, then a slide toward $75,600 before it settled back near $75,800. The range on the day has run from about $75,240 to $76,280, leaving the market little changed from its pre-release level even as rate futures repriced.

That muted finish fits the pattern after big macro prints. Most of the damage to the bitcoin price this week came from equity risk-off, not from the labor market itself, and a hot payrolls number mainly reinforces the path the Fed was already pricing.

CME FedWatch odds put roughly a 79% chance of a 25-basis-point hike at the Dec. 9 meeting after the release, up from about 70% into the open. The next employment report is due Dec. 4, five days before that decision, and the signal points the same way the last Fed hold did: stronger hiring and firmer wages leave less room for another pause.

Thursday’s session still sets the floor under the bitcoin price. The Nasdaq Composite fell about 5.5% and dropped into correction territory, more than 10% below its recent high, while the S&P 500 lost about 4.4% and the Dow shed roughly 1,800 points, or 3.4%, CNBC reported.

The Magnificent Seven erased more than $1 trillion in market value. Nvidia alone lost more than 9%, wiping out roughly $440 billion in capitalization, as investors priced in a slower AI data-center buildout after the Democratic midterm sweep.

Bitcoin has traded with that AI-heavy complex for most of the year, so a correction in the Nasdaq has pulled the bitcoin price lower even when on-chain flows looked steady. Miners that leased power and floor space to AI tenants are caught in both trades.

Fund flows followed the risk-off tape. U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs posted $430.2 million in net outflows on Thursday, led by about $200 million from Fidelity’s FBTC and roughly $105 million from BlackRock’s IBIT, bringing week-to-date redemptions through Thursday to about $870 million.

Leverage kept the bitcoin price soft into the overnight. More than $1 billion in crypto positions were liquidated over 24 hours, mostly long bets built during Wednesday’s short-squeeze bounce that lifted bitcoin near $80,000.

Professional and business services led October hiring with 70,000 jobs, health care added 42,000, and transportation and warehousing gained 25,000. Manufacturing rose 19,000. The breadth undercuts the recent pattern in which health care carried most of the gains.

Policy risk has not eased either. House Democrats are already drafting their own market structure bill ahead of taking the committees in January, a backdrop that has hung over crypto equities since the midterm results first hit the bitcoin price.

Traders are watching whether the bitcoin price can defend the mid-$75,000 zone that held on election night and several times since August. A clean break would leave September’s low back in sight after a week that erased most of the post-election squeeze, now with a hotter labor market and firmer hike odds stacked on top.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is near $75,500.

#5113. The catch in recovering a hacked crypto wallet: the thief may still have a key

Keplr’s documented backups can reduce login dependence, but they cannot establish that a potentially exposed key is safe.

A crypto wallet that lets users sign in with Google or Apple can still lock them out when its login provider stops working. Web3Auth’s security pause has exposed that dependency, alongside a more serious problem: some Keplr users’ private keys may have been compromised. In the wallet app Keplr, a previously saved, usable private key offers a documented route independent of Google login. It does not establish that an incident-affected key remains safe.

On Nov. 7, @keplrwallet reported unauthorized transactions on some accounts created with Google or Apple login and said those login methods were paused. Later that day, @Web3Auth said it had identified a security issue in its infrastructure affecting certain embedded wallets used by Keplr. The disclosures turn a question about login availability into one about what users can still control if an account's key may also be exposed.

Web3Auth's Login Service notice dates maintenance to Nov. 6 at 18:30 UTC and attributes it to a security incident. At 13:08 UTC on Nov. 8, its status page still promised an update when service was restored. The displayed maintenance window ended Nov. 7, but that endpoint did not confirm restoration.

The Nov. 7 MetaMask Builder announcement said an initial investigation found no evidence MetaMask users were impacted.

Keplr's security documentation, updated in September 2025 and still available, says Google-login accounts receive a hexadecimal private key generated through Web3Auth instead of a recovery phrase. It says the key is encrypted and stored locally on the user's device.

The same documentation describes a View Private Key option, subject to exceptions for some legacy accounts. Keplr's mobile import guide separately documents connecting with Google and importing with a recovery phrase or private key. It also tells Google-login users to save their private key securely.

A saved key therefore matters because the documented import route uses that secret directly, separately from Google sign-in. But a menu that normally offers export does not establish that every locked-out user can reach it during this pause. The documented route is not a tested workaround for this incident.

The newer Keplr guidance makes that boundary consequential. In a later Nov. 7 update, @keplrwallet described a private-key breach affecting some social-login users and said it was assisting Web3Auth's ongoing effort to protect assets. It said accounts created with recovery phrases or hardware wallets were not affected, while Google- or Apple-login accounts might be.

For those potentially affected accounts, @keplrwallet advised against sending new funds and said remaining liquid assets should be moved to a recovery-phrase or hardware-wallet account. That guidance concerns moving assets to a different account, a separate action from loading the same key into another app.

Importing the same private key into another interface can change how its owner accesses the account. It does not change the secret authorizing it or establish that nobody else possesses it. Portability can address an access dependency without resolving a confidentiality problem.

A key already usable in the existing wallet creates a different access condition from needing a fresh provider login; the documents do not test incident-time behavior.

@Web3Auth also said it and partners had begun whitehat recovery activities targeting vulnerable Keplr wallets not yet impacted, using the original attacker's path. Separately, @keplrwallet said some reduced or empty balances could reflect mitigation efforts and that staked and locked assets made the work more complex. An unfamiliar transfer or diminished balance alone cannot establish which activity occurred.

Earlier CryptoSlate coverage of the Ledger/Cosmos access outage and Phantom's Sui cutoff showed how wallet interfaces can constrain practical access.

The useful test for a seedless wallet is whether its user has a usable, independent access path before a provider becomes unavailable. During this incident, that question sits alongside unresolved account-level impact and asset recovery. Restoring login service would answer the availability question; it would not, by itself, settle either of the others.

#5112. Bitcoin holds $75K as AI regulation fears see KOSPI halted after 8% crash

Bitcoin consolidated as fears over US AI regulation spread to Asia, causing South Korea to announce measures to calm stock-market volatility.

Bitcoin (BTC) held above $75,000 on Friday as a sell-off in US technology stocks spread to Asian markets.

Data from TradingView showed BTC/USD trading near its 50-week simple moving average (SMA), at $75,733.

BTC/USD one-day chart with 50-week SMA. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

The pair fell as much as 6.6% on Thursday as US stock markets saw sustained selling throughout the Wall Street trading session. The S&P 500 and tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite closed down 4.4% and 5.5%, respectively.

Nasdaq Composite Index one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

On Friday, Asian stocks extended the sell-off. South Korea’s KOSPI Composite Index fell more than 8% shortly after the open, triggering a circuit breaker that halted trading for 20 minutes. The index then pared losses to close 6% lower at 5,059.69 points, its lowest close since March 31.

KOSPI one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

At an emergency meeting, Korea’s deputy prime minister and finance minister Lee Hyoung-il announced measures to reduce forced selling.

Brokerages will be exempt from a requirement to maintain a 140% collateral ratio on margin loans for three months from Monday, local media reported. Authorities said they would prepare futher measures if volatility increased.

“We recognize with gravity the expanding volatility in global stock markets following the U.S. midterm elections,” Chosun quoted an unnamed government source as saying.

Analysts linked Thursday’s Wall Street losses to fears of tighter AI regulation after Democrats won the House and Senate. Those fears compounded existing market concerns about the sector’s capital spending.

Related: Bitcoin, Ether ETFs post $530M outflows as election gains evaporate

Citing an X post by Democratic Rep. Yassamin Ansari, who called for a “pause on advanced AI development,” market commentary service The Kobeissi Letter said an outright US ban on AI was unlikely.

“In our view, a ban of AI will not happen in the US (with near-certainty). Such a ban, if even possible to enforce, would mean the US surrendering its position in the global AI race to China. That won’t happen,” it wrote in an X post.

Kobeissi noted that Thursday’s losses were concentrated in tech, with the S&P 500 declining just about 2.5% excluding the sector. The Philadelphia Semiconductor Index, which tracks 30 semiconductor companies, finished Thursday 12.2% lower.

Philadelphia Semiconductor Index one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Economist Mohamed El-Erian also called for common ground on AI policyt.said that lawmakers would need to find common ground on AI policy.

“America’s ‘let it rip’ approach to AI aligns with the technology’s productivity potential and national security imperatives. However, it has fallen short not only on safety and diffusion, but also on bringing society along,” he wrote in a Substack note.

El-Erian said the backlash reflected “genuine concerns,” information failures and behavioral factors.

Cointelegraph previously reported that Democrat midterm sweeps under Republican presidents have historically been followed by strong stock returns. Bitcoin’s correlation with the S&P 500, has become more positive in recent months.
