#5026. Bitcoin ETFs flip negative for 2026 after $1.28 billion outflow week, worst since June

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The 12 U.S.-listed spot bitcoin funds have now lost $914.1 million for the year, while ether, Solana and Hyperliquid ETFs all extended weekly outflow streaks in a brutal turn for crypto ETFs.

U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs logged $1.28 billion in net outflows last week, their worst week since late June, per The Block's analysis of SoSoValue data. It was the funds' second straight negative week and made the top ten largest weekly outflows since they launched in January 2024 at #7.

The funds saw outflows every day except Wednesday last week, per the data. They lost $398.6 million on Monday, $115.6 million on Tuesday, $430.2 million on Thursday and $411.9 million on Friday, with Wednesday's relatively modest $74.6 million inflow marking the only positive day.

The most recent worse week ended June 26, when the funds shed $1.79 billion in what The Block reported at the time was their second-worst week on record. Bitcoin ETFs lost $202.6 million the week before last, including a $643.3 million Friday outflow that was the largest single-day exit in four months after the record Binance hack.

BlackRock's IBIT accounted for $403 million of Friday's loss, or about 88% of the day's gross outflows. Bitwise's BITB and Ark & 21Shares' ARKB each lost about $20 million, and Grayscale's GBTC shed $11.1 million. Fidelity's FBTC took in $42.8 million on Friday, after leading Thursday's outflows with $199.9 million, as The Block reported.

The week's outflows erased bitcoin ETFs' gains for the year. The funds entered the week with about $368 million in 2026 net inflows, after turning positive for the year in late September. Monday's outflows pushed that figure below zero, and the funds closed Friday at about $914.1 million in net outflows year to date.

The total value of bitcoin held across the 12 funds dropped 2.3% to $95.9 billion, equal to about 6.3% of the cryptocurrency's market capitalization. IBIT held $60.3 billion in net assets against $65.1 billion in cumulative inflows.

The outflows came amid a crypto and equities selloff after Tuesday's midterm elections. Bitcoin fell 5.6% on Thursday alone, and analysts linked the slide to the Democratic sweep of Congress and doubts over AI spending, The Block reported Friday. Analysts say Texas's blue wave in particular could result in slowed development in the state replete with bitcoin mining facilities and data centers.

Spot ether ETFs lost $365.3 million last week, per SoSoValue data, their sixth straight week of net outflows. The run has drained about $2.02 billion from the funds since late September. Ether ETFs have twice posted eight-week losing streaks, most recently when they matched that record in early July.

Like the bitcoin funds, ether ETFs had only one positive trading day last week, also on Wednesday, when they gained $53 million. Monday's $139.8 million outflow was the week's largest, followed by $100 million on Thursday and $93.2 million on Friday. BlackRock's ETHA accounted for $90.8 million of Friday's total, about 96% of the gross losses, mirroring the firm's bitcoin fund.

The products are now down about $405.6 million for 2026. Net assets fell 2.9% over the week to $12.84 billion, against $11.92 billion in cumulative net inflows.

Spot Solana ETFs lost $26.3 million last week, per SoSoValue, nearly all of it on Friday. It was their fifth straight negative week, the longest streak since the funds launched in October 2025, and brought five-week outflows to $165.4 million. The streak began the week the funds ended a record 14-week inflow run.

Bitwise's BSOL led Friday's Solana outflows with $14.4 million, followed by Grayscale's GSOL at $5.4 million. The funds held $1.31 billion in net assets as of Friday, down from $1.40 billion a week earlier, though they remain about $676.2 million positive for the year.

The three publicly traded Hyperliquid ETFs shed $15.6 million, also extending their outflow streak to five weeks, the longest since they began trading in May. The funds have lost $58.4 million over that stretch, and their net assets fell about 17% on the week to $374.1 million. Friday's $8 million outflow was led by Bitwise's BHYP.

Bitcoin traded near $75,880 on Saturday morning, while ether changed hands around $2,244, SOL near $89.84 and HYPE around $77.79, according to The Block's price pages.

#5024. Bitcoin ETFs Bleed $430 Million as Tech Rout Tests Wall Street Buyers

Bitcoin ETFs Bleed $430 Million as Tech Rout Tests Wall Street Buyers

U.S. spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds lost $430.2 million to net withdrawals Thursday as a technology-stock selloff spread to crypto, with Fidelity and BlackRock leading the exits.

Fidelity's Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC) posted $199.9 million in net outflows and BlackRock's iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) shed $105 million, according to Farside Investors. Together they accounted for about 71% of the day's withdrawals.

Grayscale's GBTC lost another $49.9 million, while Franklin Templeton's EZBC shed $32.9 million. No fund in Farside's table recorded positive net flows Thursday, although several reported no net movement.

The latest withdrawals brought this week's net outflow to $869.8 million, following a $643.2 million outflow last Friday. Wednesday's modest $74.6 million inflow offered a brief reprieve before Thursday's reversal.

Fidelity's swing was particularly sharp: FBTC attracted $236.7 million Wednesday, then surrendered nearly $200 million the next session. BlackRock, meanwhile, lost $201.2 million even on Wednesday's positive day, extending a five-session withdrawal streak since October 30 to roughly $1.04 billion.

ETF flows have often been treated as a gauge of demand from investors accessing Bitcoin through regular brokerage accounts. The latest figures show that access works both ways: the same familiar wrapper that makes buying easy also makes exiting straightforward.

Because ETFs trade on stock exchanges, investors can adjust their Bitcoin exposure without transferring coins to a crypto wallet. That convenience can also make the funds an easy place to cut risk during an equity selloff. Daily net flows capture shares created or redeemed at the fund level, not every trade between investors.

Thursday's redemptions arrived as the Nasdaq Composite plunged 5.5% and the S&P 500 fell about 4.4%. Chipmakers and data-center stocks led the decline as investors questioned the pace of artificial-intelligence spending and potential restrictions after the U.S. midterm elections.

Bitcoin also surrendered its post-election rebound, falling back toward $76,000 after trading above $80,000 Wednesday. Short liquidations had helped power that rally, making the subsequent reversal more painful for leveraged traders.

The ETF figures add another piece to that picture. Net flows measure creations minus redemptions of ETF shares; they cannot tell whether holders were raising cash to cover losses elsewhere, trimming Bitcoin exposure, or rebalancing portfolios.

#5022. 5 Reasons to Fall in Love with La Quinta

Whether you’re visiting to play, explore, or simply chill, this desert paradise in California’s Coachella Valley has a little something for everyone.

Every day, from deciding whether or not to hit snooze to choosing to read a few more pages before shutting our eyes, our brains are a blur of choices—approximately 35,000 according to prevailing research. And frankly, it’s exhausting trying to make the right one every time. A vacation reboot would help… but travel planning requires even more decisions, which obviously defeats the purpose. And so the trip gets backburnered again.

What if there was a place that made choosing easy—because there’s no possible wrong answer? A land where you could decide to do all the things on the visitor’s bureau checklist, or none of them, and have an equally good time?

Enter La Quinta, California, a city of championship golf courses, towering palms, sprawling resorts with pools and spas, Old Hollywood lore, dramatic mountain backdrops, purple skies at dusk, and savory restaurants tucked into a calm corner of the Coachella Valley. It’s a place where you’re encouraged to do only what you love and what makes you feel most alive. Here in this sun-kissed slice of paradise, choose to follow the itinerary that is most Quinta-ssentially you.

There are 20 golf courses within the city limits, including the nine at the PGA West, a 2,000-acre luxury golf resort and community. Among those are the Mountain Course and the Stadium Course, which blend stunning natural beauty, manicured landscapes, challenging holes and expert designs by masters Pete Dye, Jack Nicklaus, and Greg Norman.

You can hit up the pickleball and tennis courts at Fritz Burns Park from dawn until well past dusk. The 12-acre green space also features a skate park, a children’s playground, a pool, and a dedicated area to play fetch with your pup. If you need to brush up on the rules of your chosen game, book some time with the pros at La Quinta Resort & Club, where you can rent equipment or pick up a match.

Whether you’re craving laid-back brunch bites, like the huckleberry hotcakes or double smashburgers at the farm-to-table eatery Beautiful Day, or a night on the town enjoying minty lamb chops and mai tais exploding with citrusy pucker at DSRT Club, La Quinta’s culinary scene does not disappoint.

Greet the day with Mexican delicacies like machaca or chilaquiles on the colorful porch at Casa Mendoza. If you have a hankering for south-of-the-border specialties later in the day, they also make a mean molcajete, seafood enchiladas, or tacos al pastor. Come afternoon, head to La Quinta Cliffhouse for the legendary nightly happy hour and stay for supper at sunset. There, you can put the date in date night by snacking on Medjools with bacon, blue cheese, coconut, and honey. (Pro tip: always order the gooey stone fruit however it’s served, whether as a shake, syrup or side dish—the Coachella Valley grows 95% of the dates in the U.S.) Close out the night with a slice of decadent Toll House Pie, a fixture at The Sandbar for four decades.

Those who believe it is just as important to nourish the mind as it is the body can take a walk down memory lane at the free La Quinta Museum, where the newly expanded history and culture exhibit details the people, technologies, and businesses that shaped the city, including the Cahuilla Indians, homesteaders, the railroad, and the celebrity magnet La Quinta Resort, which is celebrating its centennial this year. At the museum, visual learners can study the massive mural and a collection of photographs.

La Quinta itself is a patron of the arts. It’s hard to miss the more than 160 sculptures, murals, and decorated utility boxes installed through the Art In Public Places program. Twice a year, creatives from around the world descend on the civic center campus and turn it into a concert venue and an alfresco art gallery for a four-day festival. After admiring the artwork, head over to Old Town for locally made mementos. The charming retail district—known for its pleasing Spanish colonial architecture and vibrant sprays of bougainvillea—stocks gems at independent boutiques and galleries year-round and hosts the open-air Art on Main Street event on select Saturdays from November to April.

The area is a bonanza for outdoor activity. Bike along the base of the Santa Rosa Mountains using the paved, 2.7-mile-long Bear Creek Trail, or fix your attitude by gaining altitude on the miles of trails littered throughout the Santa Rosa and San Jacinto mountain ranges.

One popular route takes hikers a little more than six miles from the La Quinta Cove neighborhood to Lake Cahuilla, which is particularly peaceful at dawn or dusk. (The luckiest trekkers spy bighorn sheep.) Go straight to the lake to picnic, go horseback riding, or fish. Prolong your city escape by pitching a tent and camping at Cahuilla. Sunrise yoga and movie screenings are also offered lakeside.

While it’s clear there’s lots to do, know that, in the LQ, doing nothing is always on the table. Downtime happens to be a desert specialty, as there’s always a cold pool, a chaise lounge, and an umbrella drink calling your name. In fact, La Quinta Resort & Club boasts 42 pools across the 45-acre property, including a family-friendly splash zone and a more serene adults-only plunge with private cabanas and movie nights. (Don’t forget the 53 hot tubs for crisp Coachella nights.) Those who enjoy night swimming should post up at the never-closed pool at The Chateau at Lake La Quinta.

Finish the evening with stargazing and a glass of something sparkling in front of the fire pits. To really lean into relaxation, melt into a hot stone massage at Coral Spa or happily drift off while indulging in chromotherapy, a steam bath, or a dreamy scalp rub at Spa La Quinta. Or tap into an even deeper state of zen by joining a meditation or breathwork session or Sound Bath Sundays at Gather Yoga in Old Town. Plan your easiest trip ever today.

#5021. US 401(k) proposal leaves door open to altcoins, but hurdles remain

While the proposal doesn’t exclude altcoins and smaller cryptocurrencies, experts say they could face challenges getting into retirement plans.

Smaller cryptocurrencies could become available in US 401(k) retirement plans under a proposed Labor Department rule, but experts say they may face more challenges than Bitcoin (BTC) and Ether (ETH).

The rule reached the White House for final review on Thursday, according to the Office of Information and Regulatory Affairs. It aims to make it easier for retirement plans to offer alternative investments like crypto, while offering more legal protection for employers and plan managers.

The Labor Department’s March proposal neither specifies which digital assets could be included nor sets a minimum market capitalization requirement. However, it does guide retirement plan managers to consider six factors when choosing: performance, fees, liquidity, valuation, benchmarks and complexity.

The proposal could open the door to more cryptocurrencies in retirement plans, however, it does not guarantee they will be offered. And when it comes to smaller tokens, experts say they could face challenges because of their limited history, liquidity and other risks.

Teresa Goody Guillén, managing partner at Bellementis PLLC, said Bitcoin and Ether are likely to be the first cryptocurrencies offered because of their established markets and investment products. Bitcoin and Ether are the two largest cryptocurrencies by market capitalization, at approximately $1.5 trillion and $273 billion, respectively, according to CoinGecko data as of 20:01 UTC on Nov. 6.

Still, that doesn’t automatically exclude altcoins, she said. Instead, those managing 401(k) plans would need to show that their investment choices are sensible.

“The real question isn’t which cryptocurrencies regulators pick – it’s whether an investment meets the same standards of prudence, liquidity, valuation and investor protection as any other retirement option,” Guillén said.

She added that while a digital asset’s market cap matters, it shouldn’t be the deciding factor. “Big doesn’t mean suitable, and small doesn’t mean disqualified,” Guillén said. “Digital assets shouldn’t get special treatment, or be excluded because they use different technology.”

Bernardo Brites, co-founder and CEO of Trace Finance, echoed Guillén sentiment, adding: “Run any token through that list and the answer is short. Bitcoin passes clearly. Ether passes; everything else fails on track record or benchmark before a fiduciary ever gets to market cap.”

Brites explained, in this situation, market cap matters only indirectly. “The practical test is simpler: can a plan committee document a daily, reliable price from a regulated wrapper?” he said. “If not, it is not going in.”

While the Labor Department’s proposal does not limit crypto investments to exchange-traded funds (ETFs) or managed funds, experts said those products will likely be offered before direct crypto holdings.

“ETFs and managed funds will likely come first because they fit existing custody and recordkeeping systems,” Guillén said. Whereas holding cryptocurrencies directly would raise additional concerns about storage, cybersecurity and pricing, Guillén said. Still, she added that those risks should not automatically rule them out.

Brites also expects crypto investments to become available gradually. He said existing brokerage options would likely come first, followed by standalone ETFs and funds that include small amounts of crypto.

He expects some crypto investment options to become available in 2027, followed by ETFs at larger employers later that year. Crypto could become more widely available in retirement funds by 2028, he said.

“The rule changes the legal risk, not the plumbing, and the plumbing is slower,” Brites said. “White House review can take up to ninety days, then publication, then an effective date typically sixty days later.”

Brites further explained that legal uncertainty could also slow adoption. The proposal would offer retirement plan managers some protection from lawsuits, but it would not prevent them from being sued over their investment decisions.

He also warned that future administrations could reverse the rule, a possibility that could make employers hesitant to add crypto to long-term retirement plans.

“The 2022 guidance was reversed in 2025; a fiduciary signing a ten-year plan design has to consider that this rule could be reversed too,” he said. “And with courts giving less deference to agencies, how much weight the safe harbor carries in litigation is itself unsettled.”

#5020. Ex-FBI agent pleads guilty to stealing $2.47 million in crypto from wallets tied to 35 investigations

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Court filings show Patrick Yaroch drained at least 79 wallets, including some belonging to crime victims, and planned to use the money to buy a vineyard in Portugal.

Former FBI agent Patrick Steven Yaroch admitted on Thursday to stealing about $2.47 million in cryptocurrency from wallets tied to FBI investigations, according to the U.S. Attorney's Office for the Eastern District of Virginia.

Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, Virginia, pleaded guilty to one count each of wire fraud and money laundering related to the thefts. Each count carries up to 20 years in prison, and sentencing is set for March 4, 2027.

Beginning no later than December 2024, Yaroch searched FBI databases, including a classified one, for crypto credentials, according to the statement of facts he signed. He used the information he found to make about 100 transfers out of at least 79 wallets connected to 35 FBI investigations.

Most of the wallets belonged to people under FBI investigation, and some were controlled by two adversarial nation states the filing does not name. NBC News reported in August, citing two sources, that the adversarial nation in the original complaint was Russia. Yaroch also made at least 10 transfers from wallets belonging to crime victims in other FBI cases, and at least one from a wallet the FBI had funded for an operation.

The filing calls Yaroch "indiscriminate," with individual thefts ranging from 81 cents to more than $580,000. After each theft, he kept checking FBI systems to make sure no one had noticed.

Yaroch joined the FBI's Boston Division in 2017, working on a squad focused on one of the two nation states. From February 2025 until July 31, 2026, he was a supervisory special agent in the Counterintelligence and Espionage Division at FBI headquarters.

Yaroch had racked up significant debts before the scheme began: he had taken out more than $100,000 in loans in 2024 and owed more than $45,000 on credit cards, according to the filing.

Between Nov. 25, 2025, and Jan. 13, 2026, Yaroch moved about $93,082 to a TD Bank account and used it to pay down debts, including about $30,176 on credit cards and $20,000 toward a Lexus, per the filing. Yaroch initially said he stole the crypto out of frustration, but prosecutors wrote in an August court filing that he "should not be viewed as a selfless Robin Hoodesque figure."

On June 4, Yaroch asked ChatGPT what it would do with about $1 million if it wanted to leave the U.S. and live in a European Union country. The chatbot's answer cited his wife and child, "the desire for a 2-5 hectare estate, interest in age-worthy red wine," and named Portugal "my top choice for your specific situation," according to the filing.

On June 15, Yaroch gave two Portuguese lawyers power of attorney to register him with the country's tax authority. He also arranged a September trip to Portugal tied to his planned vineyard purchase. Prosecutors also said in the August filing that Yaroch had failed to report a 10-day trip in May, when he flew to Germany and came home from Portugal.

The prosecutors asked to keep him jailed before trial, citing about $160,000 he still held on Kraken and possible Espionage Act charges that were never filed. U.S. District Judge Anthony Trenga instead released him on a $50,000 bond with location monitoring, under conditions that bar him from using video game consoles.

Yaroch initially agreed to give agents a piece of paper that appeared to contain his seed phrase, according to the August filing. However, he later asked for it back after agents told him he had the right to stay silent and that what he said could be used against him in a criminal case.

When agents found the wallet he had pointed them to, $1.02 million had already moved out. Yaroch then admitted the funds were on the lending platform Suilend and helped them reinstall a wallet app he had deleted from his phone.

Under the plea agreement, Yaroch must pay at least $2.4 million in restitution and faces a forfeiture judgment of at least $2.4 million. He also agreed to forfeit 17,990.67 USDC and about 1.38 million SUI seized on July 31, worth roughly $1.2 million at current prices, plus the contents of his TD Bank, Charles Schwab, Kraken and BloFin accounts.

Yaroch acknowledged that the full proceeds of the scheme "are not available." He also agreed to be interviewed by Justice Department and FBI officials about his crimes.

If there's eventually a Netflix documentary about the case, Yaroch won't be profiting: any money he makes from books, films, articles or interviews about his FBI work or his case will go to the U.S. government. He must also submit any such work to the FBI for review before publication.

The Block reported in August that Yaroch had been fired on July 31 and charged with transporting and receiving stolen money. At the time, the Justice Department's Office of the Inspector General said he had moved about $1 million in roughly 10 transfers and had reported himself to two Justice Department employees on July 29.

Earlier this year, the FBI arrested John Daghita in Saint Martin over the alleged theft of more than $46 million in crypto from the U.S. Marshals Service. Onchain investigator ZachXBT alleged that Daghita is the son of an executive at a firm contracted to help the agency manage seized crypto.

A federal grand jury in Alexandria indicted Daghita on 15 counts in March, and a French appeals court in Guadeloupe approved his extradition to the U.S. in May, according to local broadcaster RCI. His arraignment is set for Nov. 12 in Alexandria, according to his court docket.

#5018. Why Women Over 50 Should Add This Underrated Carry to Their Strength Routine

“Many people think core training means crunches or planks, but one of the most functional ways to strengthen the core is by learning to resist movement,” says personal trainer Alice Jordan.

Her go-to exercise for doing exactly that? The kettlebell suitcase carry.

“Holding a kettlebell in one hand and walking with good posture forces the deep abdominal muscles, obliques, and spinal stabilizers to work continuously to keep your body upright,” explains Jordan, who specializes in strength training during perimenopause.

“For women over 50, this is particularly valuable because it develops balance, posture and stability while also improving grip strength, which is an important marker of healthy aging,” notes Jordan.

Indeed, one landmark study published in The Lancet found that lower grip strength was consistently associated with a higher risk of early death—even after accounting for age, physical activity, smoking, body size, and other cardiovascular risk factors. Notably, the researchers reported that grip strength predicted somebody’s risk of dying better than systolic blood pressure.

The single-arm kettlebell suitcase carry, adds Jordan, “also translates directly into everyday activities such as carrying shopping, lifting luggage, or playing with grandchildren, making it a highly functional exercise.”

Below, Jordan explains how to do the exercise effectively and safely.

“Aim to include the exercise 2-3 times per week as part of a strength training program. It’s particularly effective when performed after your main strength exercises or as part of your core training,” adds Jordan.

As for how heavy a weight you should use for this exercise, Jordan recommends “choosing a kettlebell that feels challenging while still allowing you to maintain perfect posture throughout the walk.”

“You should be able to resist leaning to one side, keep your shoulders level and your core engaged from start to finish,” she adds.

“As a general guide, beginners might start with around 15-20 lb, while those with more strength training experience may use 20-30 lb or heavier, depending on their ability.”

Below, Jordan highlights four key mistakes to avoid while performing this exercise.

She especially enjoys interviewing those working within the community to improve access to sport, exercise and wellness. Hannah is a 2024 John Schofield Trust Fellow and was also named a 2022 Rising Star in Journalism by The Printing Charity.  A keen runner, Hannah was firmly a sprinter growing up (also dabbling in long jump) but has since transitioned to longer-distance running. #5017. Binance CFO steps down nine days after record hack; co-CEO says transition was planned six months ago

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

After over five years at Binance, Kaiser Ng is leaving to "pursue a new venture." The firm's co-CEO said a transition plan had been in place for six months, which is long before the recent hack.

Binance Chief Financial Officer Kaiser Ng is stepping down and will be replaced by Justin Yek, a former finance executive at payments firm Airwallex, Binance co-CEO Yi He said in a statement on Saturday, Bloomberg reported.

Yi He said Ng "has decided to pursue a new venture" after more than five years at the company, and that a transition plan had been implemented over the past half year, according to Reuters. The statement did not say when Ng's departure takes effect or what his next venture is.

Ng has served as Binance's CFO since January 2022 and previously worked at rival exchange Kraken, according to his LinkedIn profile. Yek was most recently vice president of finance at payments firm Airwallex.

The announcement comes nine days after the Oct. 29 hack, in which attackers drained more than $6 billion from Binance across multiple blockchains. The FBI has attributed the attack to TraderTraitor, a North Korean hacking group.

Binance had replenished about 95% of the affected assets with its own funds as of Nov. 2, when it completed a proof of reserves snapshot and began restoring withdrawals, The Block reported. The hackers have since swapped stolen tokens for $124 million in bitcoin, according to blockchain analytics firm Elliptic.

#5015. Paraguay Protests Over Bank Lockout Intensify as Impeachment Vote Nears

Paraguay Protests Over Bank Lockout Intensify as Impeachment Vote Nears

Thousands of Paraguayans marched through Asunción this week demanding their money back after Ueno Bank, a digital lender, locked about 3 million customers out of their accounts, according.

That is about 60% of Paraguay’s adults. The bank also held $800 million to $850 million in public money, including state pension and health funds, according to the AP, so the fallout reaches the pension system.

Anger boiled over Thursday night outside Ueno’s headquarters in Asunción, where savers burned debit and credit cards. Paraguayo “Payo” Cubas, a former presidential candidate, tried to join and was booed, shoved, and forced out.

Protesters shouted “divisor” and “oportunista,” meaning divider and opportunist, accusing him of trying to profit politically from public rage, according to the same report.

Diario Hoy reported that protesters also blamed him for the Senate’s current makeup, saying Cubas put Zenaida Delgado and Javier Vera on his party’s legislative ticket, the slate of candidates voters choose from, and that both joined the government’s side soon after taking office.

#Nacionales | 😱 Echan a "Payo" Cubas de la manifestación

🔹 Paraguayo “Payo” Cubas fue rechazado por los manifestantes cuando intentó acercarse a la protesta. Los presentes lo increparon y le gritaron “divisor” y “oportunista”.

➡️ Además, cuestionaron su responsabilidad en el… pic.twitter.com/odHzVvGPO3

— Universo 970 AM (@Universo970py) November 6, 2026

President Santiago Peña, a shareholder in Ueno’s holding company until March 2025, submitted his resignation Thursday, but a joint session of Congress called to accept it adjourned without a quorum, according to La Nación. He then said he would face impeachment—a trial in Congress to remove a president—and defend himself.

The lower house had already voted 78-0 to open impeachment, and on Friday the Senate rejected his request for 72 extra hours to prepare his defense. The Senate planned a removal vote for Friday night, ABC Color reported, with Vice President Pedro Alliana next in line.

Horacio Cartes, the Colorado Party leader and Peña’s former political mentor, withdrew his support and backed the impeachment. “We will not be on the side of the plunder of public, private and retirees’ funds,” Cartes said.

Interior Minister Enrique Riera and central bank president Carlos Carvallo have resigned, and eight Ueno executives have been arrested, according to La Nación’s Friday update. Prosecutors also opened a criminal investigation into Peña’s possible responsibility for the bank’s irregularities, which he denies.

Police used water cannons and tear gas on marchers near Congress, and a new law requires most deposits to be returned within five business days, though protesters have not been placated.

The central bank says its deposit guarantee covers up to 228.3 million guaraníes per person, about $36,000, but as Decrypt previously reported, the first payouts will go only to accounts with up to 10 million guaraníes (around $1,600) and no debts to Ueno.

Bitcoiners, of course, rushed to use Paraguay’s banking crisis as an example on the benefits of decentralization and self custody, and why the “Bitcoin fixes this” motto is still relevant in today’s financial system. “Bitcoin adoption at a nation-state level can mitigate these types of situations,” said JAN3 founder Samson Mow. “If BTC is available to the people as a parallel rail, then systemic banking failures have far less impact.”

#5012. Vitalik Buterin says ETH should be valued as a store of value, not a fee-burning token

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The Ethereum co-founder said the case for valuing ETH as a fee-burning token "never was as strong as it seemed" and that maximizing fees would force Ethereum to operate like a major corporation.

Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin said he believes ether Ethereum (ETH) should be treated primarily as a store of value, arguing that the case for valuing it as a fee-burning token was weaker than it appeared during the last market cycle.

In an X thread on Sunday morning, Buterin said "ETH as store of value" makes "much more sense to focus on" than "ETH as fee-burning token." He said EIP-1559, the 2021 upgrade that burns part of every transaction fee, "is fine and good," and the network should keep the mechanism and its burn, even if the burn only partially offsets the issuance of new ETH. "But we should not feel sad if fee burn is < 10% of breakeven indefinitely; the value of ETH comes from elsewhere," he wrote.

The fee burn was the basis of the "ultrasound money" thesis that gained traction among ETH holders during the 2021 bull market. Buterin, in his thread, laid out the valuation model behind fee-burning tokens, in which a token's market cap would equal the annual burn divided by a discount rate. Under that model, a token burning $1 billion a year at a 4% discount rate would have a natural valuation floor of $25 billion, he wrote.

"But here's the problem: this was never a true floor," Buterin wrote. He said most of the ETH burned in 2021 through 2023 came from "crypto-internal use cases" whose revenue would have fallen alongside ETH's price, citing the network "randomly [going] crazy" for a "digital monkey sale" that burned $180 million in fees in a single day. "The floor argument only works if the backing of the revenue stream is external," he wrote.

(Actually, the sale Buterin referenced was for digital land in the Otherside metaverse from Yuga Labs, the creators of the Bored Ape Yacht Club NFT collection. The sale saw users paying up to $10,000 in transaction fees for Otherdeed NFTs that currently sell for around $150.)

Buterin said Ethereum now has a "better claim to this external backing" because of the growing market for tokenized real-world assets. But as a "normal stable mature business," that activity is unlikely to produce the same fee spikes, he added.

The amount of ETH burned each day has fallen sharply since then, according to The Block's data. Ethereum burned about 1.48 million ETH in 2022, worth roughly $3.6 billion, compared with about 19,200 ETH, or $43 million, so far this year.

Buterin said that justifying ETH's market cap, which he put at about $270 billion, through fee burn alone would make Ethereum comparable to a company like L'Oreal. Ether's market cap stood at $273.3 billion on Sunday morning, according to The Block's ETH price page.

Companies of that size spend heavily to keep their positions, Buterin wrote. Citing an AI estimate that L’Oreal spends about 0.58% of its market cap on research and development each year, he said matching that rate would “bankrupt the [Ethereum Foundation] almost immediately,” so it would instead require in-protocol funding of about 700,000 ETH a year, cutting validator revenue by two-thirds.

What Buterin left unstated: the level of funding received by the Ethereum Foundation (EF) is already somewhat controversial in the crypto community.

The EF's ETH sales have regularly drawn criticism from holders, including after it sold 20,000 ETH worth about $47 million to Bitmine over two weeks in April and May. Buterin said in May that he regularly hears from community members who feel the EF's actions don't match its public commitments.

The EF began staking part of its treasury earlier this year to generate yield, and in March it published a mandate describing itself as "one of many stewards" of the network. Amid a wave of senior staff departures in May, Buterin said the foundation would become "a smaller ship" that sells less ETH and focuses on work that "would not happen otherwise."

Buterin said growth in assets issued natively on Ethereum's base layer, including real-world assets, is "highly synergistic" with the store-of-value case because it expands what holders can do with ETH, such as using it as collateral.

Buterin views Ethereum as "really not just a blockchain anymore," but rather "a hybrid architecture that combines together blockchains and modern cryptography." In September, he mapped out Ethereum's roadmap through 2030 and said the upcoming Hegota upgrade is likely to be the network's last "normal" fork.

Buterin's post garnered support from other influential figures in crypto. Solana co-founder Anatoly Yakovenko said Buterin "is absolutely right that you can't focus on fees as the product." Attorney Gabriel Shapiro of MetaLeX called the thread "a 'nature is healing' moment," while Moonrock Capital founder Simon Dedic argued that ETH as a store of value would be "the biggest bull case for Ethereum. And at the same time the easiest story to sell to institutional capital."

The thread comes about five weeks after the co-authors of controversial proposal EIP-8363 withdrew it from consideration for the network's forthcoming Hegota upgrade, which will follow the behind-schedule Glamsterdam hard fork. The proposal, which would have burned a rising share of validator rewards as more ETH is staked, cited the risk a high staking ratio poses to "ETH's role as money" and drew opposition from SharpLink CEO Joseph Chalom and Aave founder Stani Kulechov. Its authors plan to hold a separate issuance process that runs through EthCC in April.

ETH traded at $2,236 as of 9:23 a.m. ET, down 0.3% on the day, according to The Block's price page.

#5011. Binance hack is ‘cyber war,’ TRM Labs executive says

TRM Labs executive Ari Redbord called for governments to take offensive action against North Korean hackers following the record $6.3 billion Binance theft.

The $6.3 billion Binance hack sent shockwaves through the crypto industry. Following a series of increasingly severe cybersecurity incidents, the attack on the world’s biggest crypto exchange demonstrated how far state-backed hackers can, and will, go.

TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence company, was on the front lines of efforts to stop the theft, releasing a report in its aftermath. The firm was early to attribute the attack to the North Korean hacking group, TraitorTrader, citing “indisputable” onchain and offchain evidence. The $1.5 billion hack of Bybit in February 2025 was attributed to the same group.

Binance said in its postmortem on Nov. 3 that attackers posed as recruiters to plant malware on an engineer’s work computer, then used a compromised internal AI tool to reach its wallet-signing service.

Ari Redbord, TRM Labs’ global head of policy and government affairs, told Cointelegraph that although the industry’s coordinated response to the attack was unprecedented, “we are only going to see much more of this.”

The speed and scale of industry’s response to the hack were among the factors that stood out to Redbord.

Tether froze 971.1 million USDt (USDT) tokens just 37 minutes after the main sweep. Combined, stablecoin issuers froze tokens worth about $2.67 billion, according to TRM. “Nothing like that has ever been done before,” Redbord said. “They were able to claw back 40% right out the gate.”

The race to catch stolen funds before hackers can move them offchain is a critical part of defending against such attacks. TRM’s Redbord explained that the Beacon Network, an intelligence-sharing system TRM launched in 2025, allowed companies to coordinate quickly to immobilize the funds before working to seize them. “Normally I see a few Beacon alerts maybe every day. I’ve seen, I don’t know, hundreds or maybe even a thousand Beacon alerts over the last few days,” he said.

Related: Ethereum devs won’t fork Glamsterdam on Hoodi until smart contract risks resolved

The push for coordination comes as Bloomberg reported on Nov. 4 that the T3 Financial Crime Unit, which TRM formed with Tron and Tether in September 2024, disbanded in late August, with sources citing slow responses and poor coordination. Tether said it would continue working with TRM through Beacon.

Although Redbord called for greater coordination, he warned that such efforts may do little to deter future attacks. He noted that North Korea’s Oct. 29 theft was equivalent to roughly a third of the country’s GDP and accounted for half of the $12.5 billion stolen by state-backed hackers since 2017. Even if attackers recover only $1 billion, the potential payoff remains enormous, he argued.

“North Korea will keep doing this as long as it’s effective,” he said.

Redbord argued that stopping such attacks requires offensive action from governments in response to North Korea’s use of hackers operating as an arm of the state.

TRM’s capacity gap report found that the gap between attackers and cybersecurity defenders continues to widen, driven by hackers’ early adoption of frontier technologies such as AI. He said law enforcement and compliance teams need AI to find and patch vulnerabilities as quickly as hackers can exploit them.

“Every time North Korea hacks, we should be hacking them back,” he continued, calling for offensive cyber operations and action against laundering networks and banks that hackers use to cash out. “This is cyber war,” he said.

#5009. Why a Hyperliquid Treasury Firm Wants to Become a US Futures Broker

Why a Hyperliquid Treasury Firm Wants to Become a US Futures Broker

Hyperliquid Strategies built its business around holding HYPE. Now it wants to help Americans trade on Hyperliquid, too.

The Nasdaq-listed treasury firm, which trades as PURR and is chaired by former Barclays CEO Bob Diamond, disclosed in its quarterly results Thursday that a subsidiary has applied to become a futures commission merchant, or FCM.

That’s the type of CFTC-registered broker that holds customer funds and handles futures orders. If approved, the unit would carry U.S. client accounts for permissioned markets built on Hyperliquid, letting Americans trade perpetual futures within a regulated framework.

Perpetual futures are derivatives with no expiration date that let traders take leveraged positions. Hyperliquid, a major onchain venue for them, still blocks U.S. users.

Its HIP-3 framework lets outside firms launch their own perpetual markets on Hyperliquid’s order book after staking HYPE. A new permissioned version, called HIP-3*, would let a regulated deployer run markets restricted to approved traders, with Hyperliquid acting as neutral infrastructure.

“We deployed $802 million this quarter and filed an application to become a futures commission merchant,” CEO David Schamis said. “Those two moves are the same bet.”

The firm held about 38.5 million HYPE at the end of September, worth roughly $3.5 billion, and more U.S. trading routed through Hyperliquid would likely mean more fees for the protocol, which uses fee revenue to buy back HYPE. An FCM would also give Hyperliquid Strategies a business of its own beyond holding tokens.

During the quarter, the firm spent $785 million buying 9.1 million HYPE and $17 million repurchasing 2.7 million of its own shares, funded largely by $911 million raised through stock sales. It reported $620.6 million in net income, driven almost entirely by $803 million in unrealized gains as HYPE rose from about $65 to $91.

It won’t be alone. Kraken parent Payward has said it plans to deploy HIP-3* markets through its CFTC-regulated Bitnomial exchange, with NinjaTrader Clearing serving as the FCM.

And last week, NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange agreed to team up with Trade[XYZ] to bring Hyperliquid-based perpetual futures to the U.S., sending HYPE up 13%. Trade[XYZ] already runs markets on Hyperliquid, including perpetual futures tied to the S&P 500.

Regulators must sign off on both the FCM registration and the permissioned HIP-3 markets it would serve, and the company has given no timeline.

#5006. Web3Auth says it frontran hackers by moving $5.6 million out of exposed Keplr wallets

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Consensys' Web3Auth team said it used a flaw that exposed some Keplr Wallet users' private keys to move their funds to safe addresses. Its 12 recovery wallets currently hold about $5.6 million.

Web3Auth, the Consensys-owned service that runs Google and Apple sign-in for crypto wallet Keplr, said it moved funds out of vulnerable Keplr wallets to keep them away from an attacker. The 12 addresses it says hold the recovered funds had about $5.6 million in crypto as of publicatino time on Sunday, according to onchain data reviewed by The Block.

A flaw in Web3Auth's systems exposed the private keys of some Keplr Wallet users who signed up with a Google or Apple account, the firms said. Though it was initially designed for the Cosmos ecosystem, Keplr now supports now supports many non-Cosmos chains, including EVM networks, and said in 2025 that it had more than 2 million users.

"This was not a hack of Keplr's code. The incident originated at Web3Auth, a provider Keplr uses," Keplr co-founder Josh Lee wrote on X, estimating that about 20% of Keplr's Google and Apple login accounts were affected. Users who set up Keplr with a recovery phrase or a hardware wallet are not affected, Keplr said.

Web3Auth said on Saturday it had started emptying vulnerable wallets the attacker hadn't reached yet, using "the same path of the original attacker." "Funds recovered through this process are safe, and we are working with Keplr on a path to recovery for users," Web3Auth said.

Web3Auth's reference to an "original attacker" suggests some wallets were drained before the recovery began. Web3Auth has not said how many wallets the attacker hit or how much was taken, and did not immediately respond to a request for further information from The Block.

"We are aware that some of these transactions have been mistaken for attacker activity," Web3Auth said early Sunday, when it published its recovery addresses. Blockchain analytics firm Bitquery had reported on Saturday that a thief collected about $6 million from more than 44,000 Keplr wallets. All eight addresses Bitquery tied to the thief are on Web3Auth's recovery list.

Most of the recovered funds are on Cosmos Hub, where Web3Auth's recovery address holds about 2.49 million ATOM Cosmos Hub (ATOM), worth roughly $3.3 million. Much of that ATOM had been staked, and the sweeps converted it into tokens that could be moved right away instead of waiting out the network's 21-day withdrawal period.

Lee said on Saturday that many vulnerable accounts may have dodged the attack. "It appears only a fraction of that 20% has actually been exploited, and the remaining accounts may be recoverable," he wrote. Keplr warned that some accounts "may already show a reduced or empty balance as a result of these mitigation efforts."

Keplr told Google and Apple login users not to send any funds to their accounts, advising them to move any remaining funds to a wallet secured by a recovery phrase or a hardware device. Keplr said it is working with Web3Auth on a way for users to check whether their accounts were affected.

Web3Auth first disclosed the issue on Friday afternoon and paused the service that lets apps create wallets through social logins. It said it had found no evidence that users of MetaMask, Consensys' flagship wallet, were affected. Consensys acquired Web3Auth in June 2025 and now markets the service as MetaMask Embedded Wallets, according to its developer documentation.

The Block has reached out to Web3Auth, Consensys and Keplr for comment.

#5002. Gnosis Pay breach exposes card details, personal data and crypto wallet information

The crypto debit card provider said customer funds remained safe despite the exposure of sensitive financial and personal information.

Crypto-linked Visa debit card provider Gnosis Pay disclosed a security breach on Friday that exposed customers’ payment card details, home addresses and cryptocurrency wallet addresses.

Gnosis Pay said it detected unauthorized access to its legacy consumer card system on Nov. 3 and began containing the breach within two hours. The company said customer funds and its separate enterprise platform, which powers white-label card programs, were unaffected.

According to breach notifications reviewed by Cointelegraph, the company identified exposed information including full payment card numbers, expiration dates, CVV codes, customer names, home addresses and wallet addresses linked to affected accounts. The compromised data also included email addresses, phone numbers, nationalities and, in some cases, banking details such as IBAN and BIC information.

Gnosis Pay customer shares a breach notification detailing exposed personal and financial data. Source: Lefteris Karapetsas

One notification also confirmed that the customer’s card was canceled on Nov. 3, with no suspicious transactions or movement of funds detected.

Gnosis Pay said it replaced compromised security credentials, addressed the vulnerability and made 3D Secure verification mandatory for online payments until the legacy system’s planned retirement on Dec. 20. It also warned customers about phishing attempts using their personal or transaction details and said it was arranging monitoring for potential misuse of the stolen data.

According to data from OpenRate, a third-party platform tracking crypto card activity, Gnosis Pay had around 2,400 daily active cardholders and processed $263,200 in transactions over the previous 24 hours.

The company did not disclose how many customers were affected and did not immediately respond to Cointelegraph’s request for comment.

#5001. Greece Passes Crypto Tax Law With 10% Gains Levy, 15% on Staking and DeFi

Greece Passes Crypto Tax Law With 10% Gains Levy, 15% on Staking and DeFi

Greece's parliament passed a bill Thursday imposing a 10% capital-gains tax on cryptocurrency profits, marking a shift from a proposal discussed in October to legislation approved by lawmakers.

The measure also puts a 15% tax on income from staking, crypto lending and decentralized finance, or DeFi.

The legislation’s crypto provisions sit inside a wider Finance Ministry package dealing with debt servicing, financial oversight, and government accounting, with the new crypto income-tax rules scheduled for January 1, 2027.

Individual investors with annual net crypto gains of €500 ($557) or less will be exempt. Cross that threshold, though, and the 10% tax applies to the entire gain, rather than just the amount over €500 ($557).

For example, an investor with €600 ($669) in net gains would face a €60 ($67) tax under the new rate, not €10 ($11). The rules generally calculate gains by subtracting the acquisition cost from the value received when the crypto is disposed of.

Selling Bitcoin for euros is one taxable event, but spending crypto on goods or services can also count as a disposal. Investors will need transaction records to establish what they paid for their assets and what those assets were worth when sold or spent.

Yield from staking, lending, and providing liquidity to DeFi protocols is treated differently. Greece will classify those returns as interest income and tax them at 15%, according to the Finance Ministry.

Qualifying crypto losses exceeding €500 ($557) can be carried forward for five years and offset against future crypto gains. The bill also gives investors a 12-month window to voluntarily declare previously unreported crypto income without late-filing penalties or interest, provided they meet its conditions and pay within 60 days of filing.

The passage follows an October public consultation on the proposal.The draft proposed a 10% rate that was already lower than the 15% levy Greek officials had discussed in June.

Athens says exchanging one cryptocurrency for another should not itself trigger a capital-gains tax. But the wording lawmakers approved may not make that exclusion as clear as the government intended.

A review by parliament's Scientific Service flagged the bill's broad language covering disposals made "for consideration." That phrase could include token-to-token trades, the review said, even though the accompanying explanation says such swaps should not create taxable gains.

The reviewers recommended an explicit exemption and suggested clearer rules for exchanges involving stablecoins and electronic-money tokens. Those details matter for traders who rotate between assets without cashing out to fiat currency.

Greece is also preparing the administrative rules needed to put the tax into practice. A senior Finance Ministry official told Reuters that guidance on transaction transparency and tax audits would be issued in the coming weeks.

The government has not estimated how much the tax will raise. Officials say the market is difficult to measure because many Greek crypto investors use trading platforms based overseas.

#5000. 7 Pendant Lights to Perfect Your Overhead Lighting Scheme (2026)

Sure, recessed lighting serves a functional purpose, but pendant lights are where things get interesting. A quick sweep through any home tour, and you’ll recognize the strategically placed Louis Poulsen above the kitchen island, an Isamu Noguchi in the entryway, and a Herman Miller saucer pendant hovering over the living room that will steal the show. Below are seven pendant light fixtures that set the mood instead of harshening the vibe. We rounded up the best pendant lights for various design personalities, including a vintage-inspired showpiece and a few variations of low-key ambient lighting. Among them are most of the different types of pendant lights you’re likely to come across, with picks for modern minimalists, Belle Époque enthusiasts, and everyone in between.

First developed in 1958, the PH 5 is a classic for a reason. Renowned Danish lighting manufacturer Louis Poulsen designed the iconic pendant with a three-shade system to equally distribute light. While the pendant works for that functional purpose, it also doubles as an unconventional art piece. Choose from a number of monochromatic and multitone colorways, including this steel blue.

Look familiar? That’s because this large pendant, inspired by Noguchi’s Akaris, hangs in many an AD home tour, including above the dining room table in this Japandi-inspired Bay Area home and in the bedroom of this South African–inspired craft home, for example. There are a few reasons why this style of paper lighting is so popular: It goes with everything; it’s affordable; and it adds a sense of levity to the room with its ethereal shape and its soft, warm glow. As lantern pendants go, this option from Design Within Reach is about as low-commitment as they come. It works anywhere you want ambient lighting rather than a spotlight.

Designer pendant lights can cost upwards of $500, but you can pay a fraction of that with finds from Article, a direct-to-consumer furniture store that sells high-quality wares at more palatable prices. That’s certainly the case with the Aino pendant, with matte colorways and a simple angular silhouette that illuminates whatever you’re chef-ing up in your Scandi-style kitchen. The metal cone shade and braided fabric cord make it an easy pick for task lighting over a counter, and if you’re working with a tighter kitchen space, there’s also a mini pendant version for $99 that ships with an LED bulb.

This three-tiered ceiling light from West Elm adds visual texture to blank space, making it a welcome addition to transitional areas in need of personality. A piece like this can also help to add structure to an open plan where the kitchen and dining rooms collide. This piece comes in 26-inch and 32-inch sizes (the larger is a proper large pendant light that can hold its own over a dining table), as well as in hardwired and plug-in options, making it one of the few statement hanging lights you can install without calling an electrician.

Any cold kitchen space dominated by stone and steel fixtures could use a touch of handmade whimsy. Hanging this handwoven rattan pendant over a kitchen island promises to warm up the whole room. Usually, two or three pendant lights hang over a kitchen island; this single shade provides a cozy, welcoming alternative, softening the light rather than spotlighting the countertop, so your mise en place won’t feel so interrogated.

This midcentury Herman Miller pendant, aptly named a saucer for its elliptical shape, is prized by commerce writer Julia Harrison for its retro, Mad Men–esque appeal. Despite the resemblance to our Hay pick above, Miller wasn’t riffing on Noguchi with this pendant: He wanted a Swedish silk-covered pendant he couldn’t justify buying, so he made his own using a sprayed plastic skin originally developed for mothballing Navy ships (whereas the orbital Hay version uses rice paper). The vertical ribbing gives it a shape that seems to be almost expanding as it hangs. Its white shade and chrome hardware make it easily adaptable to many (if not most) design styles.

This lighting fixture brings to mind 10-foot ceilings, Flemish tapestries, and an echoey marble entryway. If you’re lucky enough to have all of the above, this is exactly the sort of pendant your space needs. If not, luckily, the fixture carries that whole vibe on its own, with its cream glass bowl, brass detailing, and a sculpted metal tassel that adds a bit of movement. A vintage-inspired piece like this transforms a more humble hallway into something dignified and scenic.

Sex blankets let you get as messy as you want during sex without worrying about the cleanup afterward. While sex blankets come in a variety of styles and sizes, the best ones have a few characteristics in common. Generally, you'll want to look for a blanket that's waterproof and designed to protect your mattress topper/couch/floor/countertop from any fluids. It's also best to look for a product that's machine-washable and a great size for your needs. Ahead, sex therapists and sex educators unpack the top traits to look for in a sex blanket.

What really sets a sex blanket aside from a regular one is its capacity to reduce mess. "The best sex blanket is going to be one that has good absorption, not just something that is waterproof and doesn't allow things to get wet." She recommends looking for keywords like 'charcoal' and 'microfiber' in the product description. This will let you know it's actually absorbent instead of just a plastic-lined piece of fabric, Howard says.

Size is also an important feature to consider. Some sex blankets don’t cover the entirety of a bed, so if you’re looking to cover a lot of surface area, you'll want to opt for a larger blanket like the Liberator Fascinator Throw. On the other hand, a smaller sex blanket, like the Playdrop Sex Mat, might be more travel-friendly and easier to toss in the wash. It all depends on your needs.

If you plan on keeping your sex blanket out in plain sight, you probably don't want it to stick out like a sore thumb. "Keep in mind where the blanket will live in your space so that the color or design matches the area," Howard says. A product that matches your sheets, comforter, or pillows might also make the whole experience of using a sex blanket feel sexier and more natural.

#4995. 8 Best Online Framing Services for Displaying Art (2026)

Sometimes the most daunting part of getting art on your walls isn't tracking down a cool piece of art but finding the right frame for it. Generic online options tend to look cheap, and they’re offered in only the most basic dimensions. On the other end of the spectrum, a custom framer might quote you a higher price than the actual piece you’re framing. So, what’s an art lover to do? Luckily, there are a few tried and tested online companies that offer art gallery caliber frames at reasonable prices. Many take care of everything from printing to framing, with perks like complimentary hanging hardware to sweeten the deal. So if you’re ready to rescue those art prints still rolled up in a tube in your closet, scroll on to discover the online framing resources we trust with our precious works.

Framebridge’s offerings are more stylized than a lot of the other options you’ll find online, which is why it’s our top pick. Material options range from gilded bamboo to distressed whitewashed wood, with all frames supplied with matting, UV-protected acrylic, and hanging supplies. Either send in your artwork or upload a photograph for Framebridge to print at the desired size. “I'd been holding onto a few posters—a slick Valentino one and some vintage designs from a place in Tribeca I’m gatekeeping—when one of the brand’s numerous ads posed the answer to my framing woes,” says shopping writer Julia Harrison, who took her items to one of the brand’s physical locations (there are 44 across the country). After being walked through a different range of options and a month-long wait, Harrison told us the results were worth it. “I ended up ‘floating’ each piece I brought in—simple black frames with white or off-white backdrops for the first two, and a tortoise frame with gilded matte backing for the Robert Altman poster I brought in. I'm delighted with the final product—I feel like I'm, for once, treating my things respectfully.” The company can also frame objects such as commemorative buttons or sports jerseys that are less than 3/8" deep.

Your local craft store might be the obvious first stop for framing supplies, but that doesn't mean you have to take the DIY route. AD teamed up with Michaels, North America's largest framer, on an exclusive collection of five custom frame styles: Vintage Luxury, Accessible Contemporary, Artisanal Layers, Eclectic Maximalist, and Space-Age Modernism. Despite its big-box reputation, each frame is made to order and comes with acid-free matting, UV-protective acrylic, and hanging hardware—so it's ready to display as soon as it arrives. The collection is available through Michaels Custom Framing, with options to design your frame online or in-store. There's a frame to suit your art (and your decorating style) with options like ornate, gilded profiles and more contemporary woodgrain finishes.

Artifact Uprising will help you personalize your space with prints framed in a variety of modern styles, from floating acrylic and brass wall frames to wood tabletop frames. Each of the streamlined frames comes with hardware and hanging instructions to make it look like you worked with a professional galleryist. In fact, the brand also offers helpful guides like gallery wall decorating tips and an array of free, downloadable art to make the process feel less daunting. In addition to traditional framing, the brand’s non-frame art display options include wood photo ledges and photo hangers.

Anyone looking to create physical mementos from their digital libraries should check out Art.com, which specializes in converting digital images into works of art with just a few clicks. Options include traditional frames (with over 50 styles to choose from), canvas prints, wood mounts, and framed canvases. Customers even have access to select photo editing tools should they be looking to touch up their selections and the site will also save your images for up to three months should you be on the fence about final selects.

The framing solutions at Level Frames are handcrafted from sustainably harvested wood and environmentally friendly shellacs. Users can upload an image to print and then choose between a black or white mat and from three types of glazing. If you’re unsure of the best proportions to opt for, with a click of the golden-ratio button the site suggests the right frame based on the size of your artwork. Like Framebridge, posters and prints can also be sent to Level's studio for framing. (Note to music fans: The site specializes in framing vinyl records.)

You might not have thought it, but Etsy is one of the most affordable solutions we’ve found for custom framing. Many sellers on the site allow you to digitally send in the image you’d like to frame and the finished product will ship directly to your doorstep. If you’re looking to frame a piece of artwork or a poster you already own, many storefronts specialize in creating custom frames that will pair perfectly with your piece (just make sure your measurements are as accurate as can be).

Framers prove they know their stuff when the first question is exactly what you’re framing. Simply Framed starts with that—is it a canvas? An art print? A scarf? Next you can browse more than 35 frame choices, including sleek gallery styles or a shimmering gold leaf that can accommodate works up to 40 by 60 inches. The company can even create a shadowbox or stretch a canvas within a frame and will recommend different options depending on the medium and size of the piece. Once you’ve placed your order, you’ll receive a tube with a prepaid return label. Pop the artwork in the mail, and a few days later it should show up, framed, on your doorstep.

5” x 5” to 40” x 50” (or up to 80" unassembled)

American Frame has been in the business since 1973 and offers over 400 wood and metal frame options. The company will also print artwork and photos on a selection of nine paper types, canvas, Plexiglas, or aluminum. Customers can choose from a large variety of frames and mats and take advantage of retouching, mounting, and glazing options.

#4992. US Treasury cuts off MEXC over alleged $16B in illicit transactions

The US Treasury bureau FinCEN on Friday ordered US financial institutions to stop processing transfers involving MEXC Global.

The US Department of Treasury on Friday issued an order to cut cryptocurrency exchange MEXC Global off from the US’ financial system after accusing it of facilitating at least $16 billion in transactions linked to sanctions evasion, cybercrime and other illicit activity.

The Treasury’s Financial Crimes Enforcement Network (FinCEN) asserted that MEXC handled transactions involving sanctioned groups in Iran, Russia and North Korea between May 2021 and May 2026. This included around $1.5 billion in transactions linked to Russian sanctions evasion, according to the official press release.

The order effectively bans US financial institutions from processing money transfers involving MEXC and will take effect 30 days after it is published in the Federal Register.

“Financial institutions cannot have it both ways: Either you support the Iranian regime, or you have access to the U.S. financial system,” said Secretary of the Treasury Scott Bessent. “Treasury will continue to sever the Iranian regime’s economic lifelines and thwart its desperate attempts to circumvent U.S. sanctions using digital assets.”

The action is part of Operation Economic Outcast, a US Treasury campaign launched in August with aims to cut off financial entities that help Iran evade sanctions.

According to DefiLlama, the exchange held about $4.48 billion in total assets as of Friday. Cointelegraph contacted MEXC for comment but had not received a response by publication.

#4991. Treasury Cuts Crypto Exchange MEXC Off From US Financial System

Treasury Cuts Crypto Exchange MEXC Off From US Financial System

The U.S. Treasury Department has moved to sever crypto exchange MEXC Global from the American financial system, accusing it of facilitating at least $16 billion in illicit transactions tied to Iran, North Korea and Russia.

In an order issued Friday through its Financial Crimes Enforcement Network, Treasury said MEXC, one of the world’s 20 largest crypto exchanges, served as a major conduit for sanctions evasion and cybercrime.

The action, part of a broader effort dubbed Operation Economic Outcast, bars U.S. financial institutions from processing any funds transfers to or from the exchange. It takes effect 30 days after publication in the Federal Register.

“Financial institutions cannot have it both ways: Either you support the Iranian regime, or you have access to the U.S. financial system,” Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said in a statement, vowing to keep severing the regime’s “economic lifelines” and its attempts to use digital assets to dodge sanctions.

Founded in 2018, MEXC processes more than $1 billion in daily transactions, holds around $5 billion in reserves, and offers more than 3,000 tokens, according to FinCEN. Between May 2021 and May 2026, FinCEN said, the platform moved at least $16 billion connected to illicit activity, including nearly $1.5 billion tied to Russian sanctions evasion, with $232 million in direct transactions with sanctioned entities. The agency named MEXC a “primary money laundering concern” in connection with Russian illicit finance.

Despite publicly claiming anti-money-laundering controls and barring users in places like Iran and North Korea, MEXC’s compliance was riddled with “severe deficiencies,” FinCEN said.

The exchange was still transacting with sanctioned Russian exchange Garantex and its successor Grinex as recently as June 2026, more than four years after Garantex was first designated. It also processed roughly $200 million linked to the A7A5 stablecoin and about $22 million benefiting Iran’s central bank and its Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps.

Those connections place MEXC squarely within a sprawling sanctions-evasion ecosystem that Treasury has been dismantling piece by piece.

The department last month designated Russia’s A7 Network a transnational criminal organization and proposed cutting its front companies off from U.S. finance, flagging more than $179 billion in transactions through its ruble-backed A7A5 token, much of it routed through Garantex and Grinex.

The same campaign has reached into Iran’s crypto sector, where Treasury gained sweeping authority to sanction anyone operating in it and blacklisted the Iranian exchange Nobitex over its role in funneling crypto to the IRGC.

The order uses a provision of the Combating Russian Money Laundering Act rather than a standard sanctions designation, a tool FinCEN has increasingly reached for to cut off crypto infrastructure that operates outside the correspondent banking system.

#4984. Sony Axing Game Discs Will Have Outsized Impact on Low-Income Gamers, Experts Say

Sony Axing Game Discs Will Have Outsized Impact on Low-Income Gamers, Experts Say

Gamers in low-income areas will be among the hardest hit by Sony’s decision to stop producing physical game discs from 2028, experts told Decrypt, while one African games industry expert arguing the continent stands to lose out badly.

The secondary market has long been a cheap way for gamers to get hold of games. The PlayStation Store does run sales, but buying digitally assumes a decent internet connection, which rural households are less likely to have.

"By abandoning physical discs, some players at the bottom of the economic spectrum lose access to physical distribution through retail and, more importantly, discounted used sales," Joost van Dreunen, professor at the NYU Stern School of Business and video game business author, told Decrypt. "It won't immediately show up in the financials of major game makers, since their focus is now on creating premium experiences for a luxury class of gamers."

It comes as the cost of being a gamer is trending upwards. Sony raised PS5 prices in March, for example, citing "pressures in the global economic landscape." Similarly, the next generation of Xbox is expected to cost up to $1,800, according to Fortune.

A spokesperson for UK advocacy group Gamer's Voice told Decrypt that Sony's move to cut discs will "likely affect people on lower incomes more."

Isa Muhammad, founder of Africa and Middle East-focused games industry platform Bijetic, told Decrypt that the PS5 is already "out of reach for most Africans." He stressed that those who can buy a console still rely on the second-hand games market to afford to play on the consoles.

Well, at least the highest-income Africans could still take advantage of the PlayStation Store discounts, right? Unfortunately not. Muhammad said that the PlayStation Network is only supported in one African nation: South Africa. Many other nations are missing from the PlayStation Network's official list of supported countries.

"A gamer in Nigeria who wants to buy digitally has to set up an account registered in the U.S. or UK, then pay with foreign gift cards or a dollar card," Muhammad said.

Download speeds in rural, remote, and low-income areas are another major hurdle.

"Bandwidth is now part of what a game costs," van Dreunen said. "Roughly one in four rural [American] households has no wired provider offering 100/20 Mbps, and at 10 Mbps a 50GB download takes about 11 hours."

In Africa and the Middle East, Muhammad said, internet speeds vary significantly across the region—from super-fast connections in Dubai to unreliable ones in Iran. Internet access is also costly in some countries.

"Just recently I paid 75,000 NGN (around $55) for a 450GB broadband bundle, which is more than Nigeria's minimum monthly wage," Muhammad told Decrypt. With GTA 6's pre-load file expected to be 212GB, "a gamer here would first spend $80 on the game, then anywhere from $25 to over $60 just to download it, depending on how they buy data."

And at Nigeria's median download speed of 36.77 Mbps, per Speedtest by Ookla, the download would take nearly 13 hours. Not to mention the fact that they'd need to have a non-Nigerian PlayStation account, a download code corresponding to the country their account is supposedly from, and a way to pay in the local currency.

"Sony's own terms say you must register your account in the country where you live, and Sony can suspend or close accounts created with false information," Muhammad told Decrypt. "So every Nigerian or Egyptian on a US or UK PSN account is breaking the rules from day one."

African gamers live in fear of needing help from Sony and being refused it (or worse, banned) because their accounts break its rules, Muhammad explained. In a future where every purchase is digital, it could open the door to gamers losing accounts that hold every game they've ever purchased.

"If a player in Africa buys GTA 6 and can't recover their account, they may have no choice but to buy it again on a new one, along with everything else they've lost," he said. "A disc-free future will see this become the reality for every new PlayStation game, for gamers across most of Africa."

Sony and PlayStation did not respond to Decrypt's request for comment.

#4976. The Man Behind Fashion’s Most Dazzling Spectacles Turns His Attention to the Art of Light

For as long as he can remember, Alexandre de Betak has been entranced by light. As a 10-year-old, he rewired his Paris bedroom so that visitors had to be buzzed in, greeted by shifting colored lights. He went on to found Bureau Betak, a set design agency that transformed the runway with its megawatt spectacles for clients like Dior, Gucci, Jacquemus, and Fendi. De Betak estimates that he produced “thousands” of fashion shows—including Saint Laurent’s spring-summer 2020 ready-to-wear extravaganza, for which he bathed the Eiffel Tower in golden light, and installations for Rodarte featuring fluorescent and neon tubes.

A new truss light sculpture by Alexandre de Betak, seen here in a 17th-century Swedish castle

Now, de Betak, who sold his company in 2021, is turning the spotlight on his own creative practice. On November 5, he opened his first solo exhibition of artworks at Salon 94 in New York. The electronically programmed Truss Light Sculptures are inspired by the scaffolding and fixtures of his early runway shows and in some cases literally made from them. “That equipment is old-fashioned now, but I wanted to honor it,” de Betak says. “And I liked the irony that I spent so much energy over the years trying to hide it.”

On view at Salon 94’s Upper East Side town house through December 19, the exhibit presents two series. In the gallery’s contemporary space, the first is composed of freestanding and wall-mounted aluminum structures fitted with LED bulbs. The upstairs Stone Room, a historic space with Caen stone plaster walls and arched windows, is the backdrop for a second series of versions in bronze. To make them, de Betak had a French foundry recast each part down to the smallest screw by hand, a process that sometimes required more than 20 molds.

A pair of wall-mounted sculptures in aluminum

The light sculptures, which vary from sconce-like creations to hulking constructions out of a sci-fi movie, have a sly sense of humor and a human quality. This is especially true when they come to life, thanks to a remote-control-operated system that activates light sequences that glow, sparkle, thrum, or react to ambient noise, “whether a whisper,” de Betak says, “or extremely loud music.” For these effects, he drew on his stage work, where he was renowned for cueing, or choreographing, light and sound. “I’m interested in the ephemeral nature of what this equipment was made to show—whether a fashion show or a ballet,” he says. “At a moment when everything is virtual, I think it’s more important than ever to create things that produce a live emotion.”

Salon 94’s founder Jeanne Greenberg Rohatyn observes that de Betak’s sculptures are equally powerful switched on or off. Unlit they remind her of David Smith artworks or Russian constructivism. Lit, and with synced sound, they evoke the theatricality of stage performers. “Alex has spent his career building structures that disappear into someone else’s spectacle,” she says. “Here, the structure is the whole point.”

#4974. Suspicious prediction market activity seen before election poll release: NYT

Suspicious prediction market bets were placed shortly before two recent New York Times/Siena political polls were released to the public, according to a report.

Vanderbilt University researchers said a Polymarket user bet almost $200,000 on Oct. 31 that Nebraska Senator Pete Ricketts would be re-elected. An hour later, a Times/Siena poll was released showing Ricketts in the lead.

A Friday New York Times report said while it was not clear if the user had access to inside information, they won more than $77,000.

The user also placed similar large bets on other markets, including Kalshi, the report said.

“There’s a series of circumstances here that it’s hard to write it off as a random occurrence,” said Brett Goldstein of the Vanderbilt Institute of National Security. It “requires further investigation.”

Polymarket said it had spotted the trades and was investigating. Kalshi declined comment.

Cointelegraph reported in April that a US Army soldier had been charged after making more than $400,000 by betting on a prediction market allegedly using nonpublic information tied to a US operation involving then Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro.

In a statement, the Department of Justice alleged that Master Sergeant Gannon Ken Van Dyke was involved in the planning and execution of Operation Absolute Resolve, which led to the capture of Maduro at a residence in Caracas, Venezuela, in January.

Van Dyke allegedly used this information to buy “yes” shares in related Polymarket contracts, including “Maduro out by January 31” and “Trump invokes War Powers against Venezuela by January 31,” according to the DOJ.
